352 — Parallel Compute Scheduler from Scratch

Project Number: 352

Project Name: Parallel Compute Scheduler from Scratch

Owner: Mujir Qureshi

Document: FINAL_BLUEPRINT.md

Status: specification — locked

1. Goal

1.1 What Is Built

A single-process, multi-core, NUMA-aware CPU-bound parallel task scheduler written in Rust. The scheduler provides a fixed pool of OS worker threads each owning a lock-free Chase-Lev double-ended queue. Owners push and pop LIFO for cache-hot task reuse; idle threads steal FIFO from victim deques for coarse-grained load balancing. The system exposes fork-join (Scope/join), task DAGs with dependency-driven readiness, and data-parallel primitives (parallel_for, map, reduce, scan).

1.2 System Boundaries

Boundary In Scope Out of Scope
Process model Single-process, multi-core Cross-node / distributed scheduling (project 354)
I/O integration Blocking-task compensation threads only Async I/O reactors, event loops (project 129)
CPU arch x86-64 primary; aarch64 secondary GPU/DSP/FPGA offload
Task model CPU-bound closures, DAGs, fork-join, data-parallel Async futures / tokio tasks
Memory model C11 atomics; platform cache coherence Distributed shared memory
Observability Steal stats, latency histograms, flamegraph Distributed tracing (OpenTelemetry export)
Cancellation Structured cooperative cancellation within a pool Inter-process or signal-based cancellation

1.3 Intended Deployment Model

  • Embedded as a library crate in numerics, simulation, compiler-pipeline, or ML inference workloads.
  • Instantiated via ThreadPool::new(PoolConfig) by an application; the pool lives for the process lifetime.
  • One pool per process is the canonical deployment; multiple disjoint pools supported but not recommended.
  • Workers pinned to physical cores via CpuSet on Linux (sched_setaffinity) / macOS (thread_policy_set).

1.4 Target Users / Operators / Developers

Role Primary Interface Key Concern
Library consumer pcs-parallel / pcs-pool public API Correctness, speedup, composability
Toolchain/compiler builder pcs-graph DAG API Phase ordering, dependency edges
Systems programmer (learner) All internal crates Lock-free correctness, memory ordering
Platform operator pcs-metrics, PoolConfig Core pinning, NUMA topology, saturation

1.5 Language Rationale

Rust is required. A work-stealing scheduler lives or dies on a lock-free deque and precise memory ordering; Rust gives atomics with explicit orderings (Ordering::Acquire/Release/SeqCst), Send/Sync to statically encode which closures may cross threads, and zero GC pauses on the compute hot path. The borrow checker enforces that task closures capture no dangling references across thread boundaries, eliminating a class of data races that plague C++ work-stealing implementations. Unsafe code is confined to pcs-deque (raw pointer deque buffer) and pcs-worker (thread-local raw handle); all other crates are safe Rust.

1.6 Phase 0 Deliverable — Locked Crates

The Phase 0 skeleton defines all crate interfaces with no functional implementation:

  1. pcs-core — Task, TaskId(u64), TaskState enum, closure/job abstraction; no threading
  2. pcs-deque — ChaseLevDeque<T>, Stealer<T>; all methods stubbed; buffer allocation stubbed
  3. pcs-worker — Worker struct; thread loop skeleton; park/unpark stubs
  4. pcs-pool — ThreadPool, PoolConfig, Injector<T>; thread spawn/join skeleton; topology stub
  5. pcs-graph — TaskGraph, GraphNode, Dependency; DAG insertion stubs
  6. pcs-parallel — Scope<'s>, ParallelIterator, Producer; fork-join stubs
  7. pcs-sched — SchedPolicy enum; dispatch stubs
  8. pcs-sync — Future<T>, Promise<T>, Latch, Barrier, WaitGroup; stubs
  9. pcs-numa — NumaNode, per-domain deque groups; topology query stubs
  10. pcs-metrics — StealStats, SchedMetrics; atomic counter wrappers
  11. pcs-trace — task event spans; flamegraph export stub
  12. pcs-cli — benchmark binaries: parallel mergesort, n-body, BFS, matrix multiply

1.7 What Later Phases Unlock

Gate Phase Capability Unlocked
Phase 1 Thread pool + topology Pool boots; workers park; topology detected
Phase 2 Chase-Lev deque Lock-free push/pop/steal; owner hot path wait-free
Phase 3 Task model + steal loop Tasks spawn; steal loop runs; basic parallelism
Phase 4 Fork-join join(a,b), Scope; divide-and-conquer patterns
Phase 5 Task graph / DAG Dependency-driven task release; pipelining patterns
Phase 6 Data-parallel parallel_for, map, reduce, scan; splitter recursion
Phase 7 Scheduling policy Work-first/help-first toggle; locality-aware stealing
Phase 8 Futures / promises Future<T>::wait non-blocking; continuation chaining
Phase 9 NUMA awareness Domain-local bias; remote steal as last resort
Phase 10 Priority + backpressure External injection bounded; no unbounded memory growth
Phase 11 Cancellation + panic Structured panic propagation; cooperative cancellation
Phase 12 Blocking-task handling Compensation threads; deadlock-free blocking in joins
Phase 13 Observability Steal histograms; flamegraph; tracing sampling
Phase 14 Determinism + bench Loom model-check; criterion scaling benchmarks; soak

  • PhaseDeferred(DF-12) — priority lanes beyond two tiers
  • PhaseDeferred(DF-13) — unbounded compensation thread pool (capped at 2× workers)
  • PhaseDeferred(DF-14) — OpenTelemetry/Jaeger span export
  • PhaseDeferred(DF-15) — full loom exhaustive model checking (CI gate, slow)

1.10 Prerequisites

Domain Specific Knowledge Required
Rust unsafe, raw pointers, AtomicUsize/AtomicPtr, Ordering, Send/Sync, Box<dyn FnOnce>
Memory models C11 acquire-release; happens-before; store-buffering; TSO vs ARM
OS threading pthread / std::thread; affinity (sched_setaffinity); TLS (thread_local!)
Lock-free data structures CAS; ABA; linearizability; wait-free vs lock-free
NUMA topology libnuma; hwloc; NUMA distance matrices; cache coherence traffic
Scheduling theory Work-stealing; work/span analysis; Cilk model; continuation vs child stealing
Testing Loom (tokio-rs/loom) for model checking; criterion for micro-benchmarks

2. Why Build This

2.1 Unique Systems Knowledge

Topic What the Implementation Teaches
Lock-free deques Chase-Lev SPMC deque; Top/Bottom atomic dance; growable circular buffer; ABA on Top via monotonic counter; correct C11 orderings per Lê et al PPoPP 2013
Memory ordering Every fence and Ordering annotation is load-bearing; wrong ordering = silent corruption; teaches that SeqCst is not free and Release/Acquire pairing is often sufficient
Work-stealing theory Blumofe-Leiserson optimal work-stealing; P-completeness of fork-join; expected T∞ + T1/P makespan bound; greedy scheduling
Cache locality LIFO pop keeps youngest (cache-hot, finest-grained) tasks local; FIFO steal takes oldest (coarsest, most remaining work per steal hop) — both are deliberate and measurable
NUMA programming Domain-local deque groups; allocation affinity; cross-domain steal cost; topology-awareness at the scheduler level, not the application level
Structured concurrency Scope<'s> lifetime ensures no task outlives its stack frame; panic propagation; complete before return — the Rust lifetime makes this a compile-time guarantee
Granularity control Too-fine splits → steal overhead dominates; too-coarse → load imbalance; adaptive splitting + steal-pressure sensing teaches the tradeoff quantitatively
Blocking in a thread pool Naive blocking in join with all workers blocked = deadlock; compensation threads = avoiding deadlock without oversubscription; the interplay between concurrency and parallelism

2.2 Historical and Architectural Significance

  • Cilk (1994–1998) — first provably optimal work-stealing scheduler; showed that cache-oblivious divide-and-conquer + work-stealing achieves near-linear speedup; spawned the entire fork-join ecosystem.
  • Chase-Lev deque (2005) — the specific data structure used by nearly every production scheduler (Rayon, TBB, .NET ThreadPool, Java ForkJoinPool, Go); rare case where a single SPAA paper became universal practice.
  • Intel TBB (2006+) — industrialized the model: task arenas, task groups, parallel_for, pipeline; demonstrated scalability on NUMA servers.
  • Java ForkJoinPool (2012, Doug Lea) — the first mainstream GC'd-language work-stealing runtime; showed that the model survives managed-runtime pauses if designed carefully.
  • Rayon (2016+) — idiomatic Rust work-stealing; demonstrated that Send/Sync + lifetimes make parallel safety verifiable without runtime checks.
  • Building this from scratch reproduces the intellectual lineage from Cilk → Chase-Lev → Rayon; the implementer understands why each piece exists, not just how to use it.

2.3 Engineering Muscles Developed

  • Lock-free algorithm implementation: raw unsafe Rust with atomics; every correctness argument must be stated explicitly.
  • Cache-coherence intuition: measuring false sharing, hot-path cache footprint, alignment of deque Bottom/Top.
  • Performance debugging: perf stat, perf record flamegraphs, hardware PMU counters (LLC misses, CAS retries) to explain steal-rate anomalies.
  • API design under aliasing constraints: designing Scope<'s> so the borrow checker enforces the invariant that tasks do not outlive the scope, without runtime cost.
  • Property-based and model-checked testing: loom concurrency testing for deque correctness; quickcheck-style parallel result verification.
  • Benchmarking discipline: criterion warm-up, outlier rejection, scaling plots (1..N cores), Amdahl/Gustafson framing for analysis.

2.4 Production Engineering Concepts Exercised

Concept Manifestation
Backpressure Injector bounded; submit() blocks/returns Err when saturated (P10, I6)
Structured error propagation TaskState::Panicked; re-raise at Scope boundary (I7, I8)
Observability by default StealStats collected unconditionally at low cost; trace optional (Phase 13)
Graceful shutdown Drain-then-join or abort-then-join; no tasks leaked (I10)
Topology awareness Runtime hardware detection; affinity binding; NUMA grouping
Cooperative cancellation CancellationToken checked at task yield points; no forced abort
Compensation threading Detect blocking; inject spare worker; teardown when unblocked

2.5 Cross-Links to Portfolio Projects

Related Project Connection

Project 129 — Async I/O Reactor The counterpart for I/O-bound work; integration point: blocking-task bridge (Phase 12)

2.6 Calibration Value

An engineer who builds this can read the Rayon source, the Java ForkJoinPool Javadoc, and TBB's task_arena documentation with full comprehension. They can audit a lock-free data structure for incorrect memory orderings, explain why SeqCst on every operation is not a safe default on ARM, and instrument a parallel workload to identify whether bottleneck is steal overhead, load imbalance, or cache pressure. This project is a prerequisite for building any high-performance parallel runtime, query engine, or build system.

2.7 What Mastery Looks Like

After completing this project an engineer demonstrates the following competencies — each tied to the phase where it is forged:

  • Lock-free algorithm authorship (Phase 2): can write push, pop, and steal from memory citing the correct Acquire/Release/SeqCst justification for each atomic, then defend every ordering choice against a reviewer who says "just use SeqCst".
  • Memory-model reasoning under weak consistency (Phase 2, §5 P-C): can construct a happens-before graph for a given interleaving of pop and steal, identify the exact fence that closes the ordering gap, and predict which hardware (x86 TSO vs ARM) would expose the bug without it.
  • Work-stealing scheduler internals (Phase 3, Phase 7): can size a thread pool for a given workload using the Blumofe-Leiserson T1/P + T∞ bound, explain why LIFO pop preserves cache locality while FIFO steal maximizes stolen work, and tune granularity using measured steal-cost data.
  • Structured-concurrency API design (Phase 4, Phase 11): can design a Scope<'s> API whose lifetime guarantees are enforced by the Rust borrow checker with no runtime overhead, articulate why PhantomData<&'s ()> is invariant, and prove it prevents use-after-scope by construction.
  • DAG dependency scheduling (Phase 5, §8.6): can implement pending_deps countdown propagation with correct AcqRel fetch_sub, explain why the final decrement must use prev == 1 rather than load + compare, and extend the design for cancellation without a global lock.
  • NUMA-aware data partitioning (Phase 9, §8.10): can partition a parallel workload's data across NUMA nodes, measure cross-domain steal ratio using pcs-metrics, and interpret a numastat diff to diagnose whether slowdown is task migration or data migration.
  • Concurrent system debugging under loom (Phase 14, §5 R9): can write a loom test that exhausts all orderings for a 2-worker steal race, interpret a loom violation counterexample, and trace it back to the missing fence using the happens-before lattice.
  • Performance attribution from first principles (Phase 13–14, §5 B7–B8): given a flamegraph and perf stat output from a real parallel run, can attribute throughput loss to steal overhead, NUMA penalties, or false sharing — then propose and measure a targeted fix.

3. Architectural Overview

3.1 Canonical Diagram

                user code: spawn / join / scope / parallel_for / task graph
                                       |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |                          ThreadPool (pcs-pool)                          |
  |   hardware topology / NUMA domains / CpuSet affinity / Injector (global) |
  |                                                                         |
  |   Worker 0            Worker 1                       Worker N-1          |
  |  +-----------+       +-----------+                  +-----------+        |
  |  | Chase-Lev |       | Chase-Lev |                  | Chase-Lev |        |
  |  | deque     |       | deque     |       ...        | deque     |        |
  |  | LIFO pop  |       |           |                  |           |        |
  |  | (owner)   |<------+ steal FIFO +----------------->|           |        |
  |  |           | steal   (thief; victim select; rng)   |           |        |
  |  +-----+-----+       +-----+-----+                  +-----+-----+        |
  |        | run task          | run task                     | run task     |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------------+
        ^                       ^                              ^
        | fork-join             | data-parallel                | DAG ready-set
  +-----+-------+      +---------+---------+         +----------+-----------+
  | Scope/join  |      | parallel_for /    |         | TaskGraph (pcs-graph)|
  | (pcs-       |      | map/reduce/scan   |         | deps -> pending_deps |
  |  parallel)  |      | splitters         |         | -> Ready -> deque    |
  +-------------+      +-------------------+         +----------------------+
        |                                                     
        v                                                     
  +-------------------+     +-----------------------+
  | Future / Promise  |     | StealStats / metrics  |
  | Latch / Barrier   |     | task trace -> flame   |
  | (pcs-sync)        |     | (pcs-metrics/trace)   |
  +-------------------+     +-----------------------+

3.2 Subsystem Descriptions

ThreadPool / pcs-pool. Central coordinator. Owns N worker thread handles, a global Injector<Task> for external/overflow submissions, PoolConfig (thread count, stack size, SchedPolicy, affinity), and the result of hardware topology detection (CpuTopology: core IDs, NUMA node membership, cache sizes). Data in: Task submissions from user code and from the Injector. Data out: tasks distributed to per-worker deques; wakeup signals to parked workers. Concurrency boundary: Injector is MPMC; topology is read-only after construction; worker handles are owned exclusively. No persistence boundary; pool state is purely in-process.

Worker / pcs-worker. One OS thread per worker, pinned to a physical core via CpuSet. Owns a ChaseLevDeque<Task> (local deque, private push/pop); holds an array of Stealer<Task> handles into every other worker's deque. Execution loop: pop own deque (LIFO) → try steal from a victim (FIFO, victim chosen by XorShift RNG biased to NUMA-local peers) → park (OS futex) on empty. Unparked by the thread that pushes a task. Thread-local CURRENT_WORKER: *const Worker for fast self-identification during recursive spawns. Concurrency boundary: deque Bottom is accessed only by owner; Top is contested by thieves via CAS.

ChaseLevDeque / pcs-deque. Lock-free circular buffer of task pointers. Bottom: AtomicUsize (owner's write cursor), Top: AtomicUsize (thieves' steal cursor, monotonically increasing). Owner push: write slot, Release fence, increment Bottom. Owner pop (LIFO): decrement Bottom tentatively, SeqCst fence to race with steals, CAS on Top only if single-element contention. Thief steal (FIFO): read slot at Top, CAS Top from old to old+1; fails → retry or abort. Growable: when Bottom - Top == capacity, allocate 2× buffer, copy, publish via AtomicPtr; old buffer deferred-freed when all stealers have passed the epoch. ABA prevented because Top is monotonically increasing (never wraps in practice for u64). Data in: Task pushed by owner or injected. Data out: Task popped by owner or stolen by thief.

pcs-graph / TaskGraph. Maintains a DAG of GraphNode entries. Each node: { task: Task, pending_deps: AtomicUsize, successors: SmallVec<NodeId> }. add_edge(pred, succ) increments succ.pending_deps. On pred completion, for each successor decrement pending_deps; if it reaches 0, push to the completing worker's local deque (locality hint). Cycle detection at add_edge time via DFS reachability check (offline, before execution). Completion latch signals when all nodes are Done. Data in: node insertion + edge insertion; task-done notifications from worker loop. Data out: Ready tasks pushed to worker deques.

pcs-parallel / Scope + Data-parallel. Scope<'s> borrows the calling stack frame's lifetime; spawn pushes a task carrying a 's-scoped closure; Scope::wait blocks until pending: AtomicUsize reaches 0 (using Latch). Panic in any scope task poisons the scope; wait re-raises via std::panic::resume_unwind. ParallelIterator wraps a Producer (range or slice) with split_at(mid) -> (L, R); recursive halving spawns right half, processes left half; leaf threshold controlled by SchedPolicy. Adaptive: if steal pressure is low (deque non-empty), split coarser. Data in: user closures + data ranges. Data out: tasks pushed to local deque; results collected into output buffer or reduction accumulator.

pcs-sync / Future + primitives. Promise<T> is the write end (fulfills one Future<T>). Future<T>::wait does not block the OS thread: it runs tasks from the local deque (work-helping) until the latch fires. Latch = AtomicUsize counting down to 0 with a parked-waiters list. Barrier coordinates N parties. WaitGroup tracks in-flight task count for pool quiescence. Concurrency boundary: Promise::fulfill is a once-write; Future::wait is poll-then-help loop with no spinlock.

pcs-sched / SchedPolicy. Stateless policy object consulted by the worker loop at three decision points: (1) victim selection order (random, NUMA-local-first, round-robin); (2) continuation disposition (work-first: push continuation, run child; help-first: push child, run continuation); (3) granularity threshold for splitters. Owns no mutable state; parameterized by PoolConfig::policy. SchedPolicy is chosen at pool construction time and does not change at runtime (no dynamic adaptation yet; deferred to Phase 7 adaptive tuning).

pcs-numa / NUMA domains. Groups workers into NumaDomain sets based on topology query (hwloc or /sys/devices/system/node). Each domain has its own sub-injector for locality-preserving overflow. Worker::steal tries within-domain stealers first (numa_local_stealers), then cross-domain (remote_stealers), reducing cross-socket cache-coherence traffic. Allocation hint: task closures allocated on the worker's NUMA node (numactl-style). Data flow: topology is immutable after pool construction; domain membership encoded in Worker::numa: NumaNode.

pcs-metrics / pcs-trace. SchedMetrics: per-worker StealStats (steals_attempted, steals_won, tasks_run, park_count, idle_ns) stored as AtomicU64; aggregated on demand via metrics_snapshot(). Per-task spans in pcs-trace capture (task_id, worker_id, enqueue_ns, start_ns, done_ns); written to a ring buffer; flushed to flamegraph JSON on demand. Sampling flag (TRACE_SAMPLE_RATE) controls overhead: 0 = disabled, 1 = every task, N = 1-in-N. Concurrency boundary: all metrics writes are atomic; trace ring buffer is per-worker (no contention on write path).

4. System Constraints

4.1 Quantified Engineering Targets

Constraint Target Notes / Rationale
Worker thread count 1 – 512 Practical: 1–64 on commodity hardware; 512 upper bound for large NUMA systems
Task closure size ≤ 256 bytes inline; heap-boxed otherwise Affects deque slot size and cache line packing
Deque capacity (initial) 256 slots Power-of-2; grows 2× on overflow; max 2²⁰ slots (1M tasks in-flight per worker)
Deque push/pop latency (owner, uncontended) < 20 ns p99 Wait-free path; no CAS; cache-line aligned Bottom
Steal latency (successful, uncontended) < 50 ns p99 Single CAS on Top; victim deque hot in L3
Steal attempt overhead (failed) < 10 ns Empty-deque fast path; no CAS issued
Fork-join spawn overhead < 100 ns per spawn Push to local deque + optional unpark
Task dispatch latency (enqueue → first-execute) < 500 ns p99 under load Includes possible unpark delay
Parallel-for granularity threshold ≥ 10 µs task body Below this, steal overhead dominates; enforced by splitter
Steal success rate (at saturation) > 60% Below 50% indicates victim starvation or over-decomposition
Worker park/unpark latency < 5 µs p99 Linux futex FUTEX_WAKE; macOS __ulock_wake
Pool startup time < 10 ms for 64 workers Topology detection + thread spawn + affinity bind
Pool shutdown time (graceful drain) < 100 ms All workers join within deadline; in-flight tasks finish
Memory per worker (baseline) < 128 KB Deque buffer (32 KB at 256 slots × 128 B) + thread stack (64 KB min)
Global Injector overhead < 200 ns enqueue p99 MPMC queue; expected contention low under normal use
DAG node insertion O(1) amortized Vec append; no re-sort; cycle check is offline pre-execution
DAG completion propagation O(fan-out) per node Decrement + conditional push per successor; no global lock
Trace ring buffer size 64 K spans per worker ~3.2 MB per worker at 50 B/span; configurable
Metrics snapshot latency < 1 µs Atomic reads of per-worker counters; no stop-the-world
Flamegraph export latency < 50 ms for 1M spans Offline; does not block scheduler hot path
CPU cache assumptions L1: 32–64 KB, L2: 256 KB–1 MB, L3: shared per socket Deque head/tail on separate cache lines (false-sharing prevention)
NUMA cross-domain steal penalty ≤ 3× same-domain steal cost Acceptable; cross-domain steal is last resort
Max in-flight tasks (backpressure threshold) 4× num_threads Above this, submit() returns Err(Backpressure); caller must retry
Cancellation response latency < 1 task granularity Token checked at task entry + at user-inserted yield points
Panic propagation latency < 1 ms after task panics TaskState::Panicked → scope poisons → wait() re-raises
Compensation thread limit min(8, num_threads) extras Caps oversubscription; excess blocking tasks queue behind token
Deterministic mode overhead ≤ 2× sequential runtime Single-thread replay; useful only for debugging, not production
Loom model-check bound ≤ 3 threads, ≤ 8 operations Feasible exhaustion; captures all deque ordering bugs at small scale
Benchmark scaling efficiency (8 cores) ≥ 6× speedup on mergesort / matrix-multiply Validates steal rate and granularity tuning
Benchmark scaling efficiency (16 cores) ≥ 11× speedup Acceptable efficiency accounting for memory bandwidth saturation

4.2 Cache Layout Constraints

Structure Alignment Rationale
ChaseLevDeque Bottom Sole occupant of cache line 0 (64 B) Owner-only write; shared line → false sharing with Top reads
ChaseLevDeque Top Cache line 1 (offset 64) Contested by thieves; separate from owner's Bottom
Worker struct repr(align(64)) Prevent adjacent workers' state from sharing a cache line
Task inline closure buffer ≤ 3 cache lines (192 B max) Fits in L1 after pop; larger closures heap-boxed
StealStats per-worker Per-worker, not shared Atomic increments stay local; aggregated only at snapshot

4.3 Failure and Recovery Constraints

Failure Mode Budget / Recovery Objective
Worker thread panic (non-task) Pool detects via JoinHandle; restarts worker within 1 ms; tasks on that deque migrated via injector
All workers blocked in join Compensation thread injected within 1 steal-timeout cycle (< 1 ms)
Task panic Propagated to owning Scope; sibling tasks cancelled cooperatively within 1 task-granularity
Injector full (backpressure) Caller receives Err(Backpressure) synchronously; no data loss
NUMA topology detection failure Falls back to single-domain flat topology; log warning
Deque buffer allocation failure alloc::handle_alloc_error; unrecoverable by design (OOM = abort)
Shutdown timeout (tasks non-responsive) Hard abort after configurable deadline (default 5 s); all workers force_join

5. Required Reading

Books

# Title / Author Chapters Concepts to Extract Applied In §/Phase
B1 The Art of Multiprocessor Programming — Herlihy & Shavit (2ed, 2020) Ch. 10 (concurrent queues), Ch. 16 (work-stealing), Ch. 15 (spin locks / memory models) ABA prevention via monotonic counters; linearizability definitions; CAS semantics; why lock-free ≠ wait-free Phase 2 (ChaseLevDeque ABA analysis, I3); Phase 3 (steal loop CAS retry); §7 (P2 owner wait-free contract)
B2 Programming Rust — Blandy, Orendorff, Tindall (2ed) Ch. 19 (concurrency), Ch. 22 (unsafe), Ch. 20 (async) Rust ownership as correctness proof for single-owner invariant I1; raw-pointer discipline inside ChaseLevDeque Phase 2 (pcs-deque unsafe buffer); Phase 4 (Scope<'s> PhantomData variance, I8); §11 (Core Schema types)
B3 Rust Atomics and Locks — Mara Bos (O'Reilly 2023) Ch. 2 (atomics), Ch. 3 (memory ordering), Ch. 7 (lock-free), Ch. 9 (building locks) Acquire/Release/SeqCst selection; the store-buffer model; why consume is broken on x86; mapping C11 orderings to Rust Ordering; practical fence placement Phase 2 (every Ordering in pcs-deque, §8.3 memory-ordering table); Phase 3 (AtomicTaskState CAS); §4 cache-layout constraints
B4 Structured Parallel Programming — McCool, Robison, Reinders (2012) Ch. 4 (parallel patterns), Ch. 7 (reduce/scan), Ch. 11 (task graphs) Prefix-scan parallelism; reduce associativity requirement (I9); DAG dependency representation matching TaskGraph Phase 6 (parallel_scan two-pass design, I9); Phase 5 (TaskGraph pending_deps model); §8.7 data-parallel splitter model
B5 Introduction to Algorithms — Cormen et al. (CLRS 4ed) Ch. 26–27 (multithreaded algorithms, work-span analysis) Work-span model; greedy-scheduler bound T∞ ≤ Tp ≤ T1/P + O(T∞); apply to Phase 6 splitters Phase 6 (granularity threshold derivation, P6); Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model (makespan bound); Hard Questions (Self-Examination)
