6. Design Principles

P1. Work-Stealing over a Central Queue

Statement. Each worker owns a private ChaseLevDeque; tasks flow between workers only via steal, not through a shared queue.

Rationale. A single global queue requires a lock or CAS on every push/pop, serializing all workers. With N workers at high throughput, the queue becomes the bottleneck at O(N) contention per operation. Per-worker deques reduce hot-path contention to O(1) — push/pop touch only the owner's bottom pointer.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Near-linear scaling to high core counts; no single chokepoint.
  • (+) Cache locality: owner always pops its own recent work.
  • (-) Load imbalance requires stealing; steal itself is O(1) but has higher constant than local pop.
  • (-) Empty-deque detection (worker idle) requires polling all stealers before parking.

Operational implications. Victim selection strategy (random, NUMA-local, round-robin) determines steal latency distribution. Poor victim selection under skewed load leads to thundering-herd steals. pcs-worker uses XorShift RNG for randomness + NUMA-local bias (Phase 7, P8).

Violation consequence. If a global Mutex<VecDeque<Task>> replaces per-worker deques, throughput flattens at ~4 cores on any micro-benchmark; profiling shows 80%+ time in lock contention.

P2. Lock-Free Owner Hot Path

Statement. push and pop on the owner thread are wait-free (no CAS, no retry); only steal uses CAS and may retry.

Rationale. The owner executes push/pop at task-spawn frequency (potentially millions/s). A blocking or retry-based owner path introduces unbounded latency spikes. Thieves are rare relative to owner operations; CAS retry on steal is acceptable.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Predictable owner latency; suitable for latency-sensitive fork-join workloads.
  • (-) Owner push requires a relaxed store to bottom; pop requires a fence before the top load — the ordering is subtle (P-C paper mandatory).
  • (-) Correct implementation requires unsafe Rust; any ordering mistake causes lost or double-executed tasks (violates I1, I3).

Operational implications. In pcs-deque, push stores to the circular buffer then releases-stores to bottom; pop loads bottom, decrements, acquires-loads top, may restore bottom on contention. The single dangerous case (one item remaining, owner and thief race) resolves via CAS; owner yields the item on loss. Map each operation to the exact orderings from P-C before changing any Ordering.

Violation consequence. Replacing owner's stores with SeqCst for "safety" adds an MFENCE on x86 to every push — 30–60 cycle penalty per task spawn; visible as 3–5× throughput drop in the deque micro-benchmark.

P3. Locality by Construction

Statement. Owner pops LIFO (most-recently-pushed); thieves steal FIFO (oldest item). This is not configurable — it is structural.

Rationale. Most-recently-pushed task was just split from the parent; its data is in L1/L2 cache. The oldest task is the coarsest — it has the most remaining work, making it worth the steal cost. LIFO/FIFO emerges from the deque structure naturally (pop from bottom, steal from top).

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Maximises cache hit rate for the common case (owner runs its own recent work).
  • (+) Steal is always the "biggest" available work unit, maximizing stolen work per steal round-trip.
  • (-) FIFO steal means thieves may get coarse tasks that spawn many children — this is desired but increases deque depth transiently.
  • (-) Strict LIFO can delay long-latency tasks if they are always displaced by newer spawns (straggler risk — addressed by Phase 10 priority lanes).

Operational implications. Granularity control (P6) is essential: if every task is leaf-sized, LIFO/FIFO lose their meaning. Data-parallel splitters (Phase 6) must size leaves so that the deepest item in any worker's deque is still worth stealing.

Violation consequence. Using FIFO for the owner (ring buffer dequeue from head) turns the deque into a FIFO queue; owner's cache locality collapses; benchmark shows 2–4× more LLC misses.

P4. Never Oversubscribe

Statement. The pool has exactly PoolConfig::num_threads runnable OS threads; task-level parallelism comes from many tasks sharing those threads, not from spawning extra threads per task.

Rationale. Each OS thread beyond the hardware core count causes context-switch overhead and cache churn. Oversubscription at N+k threads wastes proportional k/N fraction of CPU bandwidth to scheduling.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Deterministic OS thread count; predictable cache footprint.
  • (-) A blocking task (I/O, lock) wastes a thread slot — requires explicit Phase 12 compensation mechanism.
  • (-) External libraries that spawn their own threads (rayon, tokio) inside pcs tasks cause oversubscription unless nested scheduling is disabled.

Operational implications. PoolConfig::num_threads defaults to num_cpus::get_physical(). Detect and log oversubscription at startup if a thread-count environment variable exceeds logical cores. Phase 12 compensation threads are temporary and bounded; they do not permanently inflate the pool.

Violation consequence. Spawning one OS thread per task in a 1M-task workload: OS scheduler overhead dominates, throughput collapses, and RSS grows by O(tasks × stack_size).

P5. Structured Concurrency

Statement. Every spawned task is owned by a Scope or a join; the scope/join does not return until all owned tasks are terminal (Done or Panicked). No detached tasks.

Rationale. Detached tasks have no owner to propagate panics, collect results, or guarantee ordering. Structured concurrency makes task lifetimes lexically bounded, enables safe borrowing across spawns (I8), and ensures I7 (panic propagation).

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Safe Scope<'s> borrows (lifetime 's bounds spawned closures to the scope's stack frame).
  • (+) Deterministic cleanup: scope drop ≡ all tasks done.
  • (-) Cannot express "fire and forget" patterns without explicit detach API (which pcs intentionally omits).
  • (-) Recursive scopes must not block the calling thread — the calling thread must participate in task execution while waiting (I4), or risk deadlock.

Operational implications. Scope::spawn borrows 's; Rust's borrow checker rejects use-after-scope automatically. Scope's internal latch is decremented by each task's completion; scope blocks by executing tasks until latch == 0 (not by thread sleep).

Violation consequence. Allowing detached tasks: a panic in a detached task is silently swallowed; the spawned closure may outlive stack locals it borrowed, causing UB in safe Rust (requires unsafe escape hatch — a red flag in code review).

P6. Granularity Control

Statement. A task's compute cost must significantly exceed the cost of one steal operation (~50–200 ns). Split data-parallel work recursively only until leaf cost ≥ granularity threshold.

Rationale. Steal overhead dominates for tiny tasks. Scheduling overhead is amortized over task compute. Under-granulation causes pathological steal rates with near-zero useful compute per steal.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Coarser tasks → lower steal rate → less deque contention.
  • (-) Too-coarse tasks → load imbalance if one worker gets a long task near end of computation (straggler).
  • Optimal granularity is workload-dependent; pcs-sched auto-tunes via steal-pressure feedback (Phase 7).

Operational implications. Default threshold: 1024 elements for numeric parallel_for; 8192 elements for scan/reduce. Expose PoolConfig::min_granularity override. Phase 6 splitters halt recursion when len() ≤ threshold. Measure steal rate via StealStats; if steals_won / tasks_run > 0.1, consider increasing threshold.

Violation consequence. Splitting a 10M-element array down to single-element tasks: 10M task allocations + 10M potential steals; memory allocator becomes the bottleneck; throughput drops 10–100× vs sequential for small element types.

P7. Composability without Deadlock

Statement. Blocking inside a task (waiting on a mutex, I/O, or a nested join) is handled explicitly by Phase 12 compensation; no hidden assumption that tasks are always non-blocking.

Rationale. Library code composed into tasks may block. If all N worker threads block in a join waiting for sub-tasks that are queued but never run (no worker available), the pool deadlocks. Compensation injects a temporary thread to unblock the pool.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Arbitrary task composition without deadlock.
  • (-) Compensation threads temporarily oversubscribe (P4 tension); bounded by PoolConfig::max_compensation_threads.
  • (-) Detection of "about to block" requires instrumentation — Future::wait and Scope::wait cooperate automatically; std::sync::Mutex::lock does not without a wrapper.

Operational implications. Provide pcs_pool::blocking(|| { ... }) wrapper that notifies the pool before the block and after return. Compensation thread is returned to a reserve pool after unblocking. Log a warning if compensation thread count exceeds num_threads (deadlock risk indicator).

Violation consequence. Removing compensation: a parallel tree traversal where leaf tasks acquire a shared mutex deadlocks if all workers are blocked inside lock() with no thread to run the unblocking task.

P8. No Global Synchronization on the Steal Path

Statement. Victim selection and steal execution touch exactly two workers' state (stealer and victim deque); no pool-global lock, no global counter increment, no barrier.

Rationale. A global lock on steal serializes all concurrent steals, eliminating the O(1) steal scalability. Even a global atomic increment (e.g., a "steal attempt" counter per steal) adds a cache-coherence round-trip at O(N) frequency.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Steal scales with number of concurrent steal pairs, not with total worker count.
  • (-) Worker parking/unparking requires a signal to wake idle workers — this is a one-time event per task injection, not per steal.
  • (-) Metrics (StealStats) accumulate per-worker atomics; aggregation is deferred to reporting time (not on the steal path).

Operational implications. StealStats uses per-worker AtomicU64 for steals_attempted / steals_won; aggregation sums across workers lazily. Global Injector<Task> is only touched for external submission or overflow — not during internal work redistribution.

Violation consequence. Adding a Mutex<GlobalStealLog> to record every steal attempt for tracing: steal throughput drops by 50%+ at 16 cores; mutex shows as top contender in perf lock output.

P9. Determinism on Demand

Statement. A single-threaded deterministic replay mode exists, producing identical task execution order across runs for the same input.

Rationale. Parallel bugs (races, non-deterministic reductions, incorrect dependency edges) are only reproducible if the execution order can be fixed. A deterministic mode sacrifices parallelism for debuggability.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Enables bisection debugging of incorrect results in parallel computations.
  • (+) loom model-checking in Phase 14 uses a similar single-threaded permutation model.
  • (-) Deterministic mode is single-threaded: no performance measurement valid in this mode.
  • (-) Requires conditional compilation (#[cfg(feature = "deterministic")]) or runtime flag; must not impose overhead in production path.

Operational implications. Deterministic mode: one thread, LIFO deque replaced by a Vec<Task> stack, no stealing, tasks executed depth-first in spawn order. Enable via PoolConfig { policy: SchedPolicy::Deterministic }. XorShift RNG seed is fixed. Phase 14 conformance tests run both modes and assert equal results (for exact-arithmetic workloads).

Violation consequence. Without deterministic mode, a parallel BFS producing different vertex orderings each run makes it impossible to write regression fixtures; every CI run produces different output, masking correctness regressions.

P10. Backpressure at the Boundary

Statement. External task submission via ThreadPool::submit() blocks or returns Err(Backpressure) when in-flight task count exceeds PoolConfig::max_inflight.

Rationale. Unbounded submission causes unbounded memory growth in the Injector queue and deques. A slow consumer (all workers busy with long tasks) paired with a fast producer leads to OOM without backpressure.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Bounded memory footprint regardless of producer speed.
  • (+) Pushes flow-control responsibility to the caller (correct design).
  • (-) Blocking submit() can cause caller-thread deadlock if caller is itself a pool worker (re-entrant submission — detect and reject with clear error).
  • (-) Throughput measurement requires a fast-consumer workload to avoid measuring backpressure overhead.

Operational implications. submit() checks an AtomicUsize inflight_count; if ≥ max_inflight, either block on a Condvar or return Err. Decrement inflight_count on task completion. Expose PoolConfig::max_inflight defaulting to num_threads × 256. Emit SchedMetrics::backpressure_events counter.

Violation consequence. Removing the max_inflight bound: a producer generating 100M tiny tasks fills heap in seconds; the allocator OOMs before any task completes; pool is unrecoverable without restart.

P11. Zero-Cost When Disabled

Statement. Every optional subsystem (metrics, tracing, NUMA awareness, determinism mode) compiles to a no-op or a single branch on the hot path when its feature flag is off; the base scheduler never pays for observability or portability it does not use.

Rationale. A parallel scheduler's hot path (spawn, pop, steal) runs at sub-microsecond frequency; any unconditional instrumentation compounds into measurable throughput loss at scale. Rust's zero-cost abstraction discipline (monomorphization, #[cfg(feature = ...)] compile-time gating) lets optional capability ship without a runtime tax for users who do not enable it.

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Users who need only the bare scheduler pay nothing for metrics/trace/NUMA code paths — binary size and branch count shrink.
  • (+) Feature combinations are tested explicitly (CI matrix), preventing silent cross-feature interaction bugs.
  • (-) Feature-gated code multiplies the test matrix (cargo test --features X,Y,Z combinatorics); Phase 14 CI must cover the default and full-feature builds at minimum.
  • (-) Conditional compilation can hide a bug that only appears in one feature combination; mitigated by testing the two extremes plus the default profile.

Operational implications. pcs-metrics and pcs-trace are optional crates behind numa, metrics, trace feature flags (ADR-26); when disabled, StealStats calls compile to nothing (empty inline function, verified via cargo asm). NUMA detection falls back to a single-domain stub when the numa feature is off, at zero runtime cost beyond one if false branch eliminated by the optimizer.

Violation consequence. Making pcs-trace span recording unconditional (no feature gate): every task spawn pays a ring-buffer write even when no consumer ever reads it; benchmark shows 15–20% throughput loss on the steal_throughput micro-benchmark with tracing compiled in but unused.

P12. Observability Is Load-Bearing, Not an Afterthought

Statement. Every invariant in this design (I1–I12) has a corresponding metric or trace signal that can prove the invariant holds (or catch its violation) in production, not only in test.

Rationale. A scheduler bug (lost task, deadlock, starvation) that only a debugger can see is a scheduler nobody can operate at scale. Designing the metric alongside the invariant — not retrofitting it after an incident — ensures the signal actually distinguishes the failure mode it claims to detect (Operational Readiness's Metrics Strategy formalizes this pairing).

Tradeoffs.

  • (+) Every failure class in the Failure Taxonomy has a paired detection signal by construction, not by after-the-fact patching.
  • (+) New invariants added later are incomplete until their telemetry column is filled in — this is enforced structurally by the System Invariants table's own schema (ID / Statement / Why / Enforced In / Detection / Telemetry / Recovery / Debugging).
  • (-) Every new invariant adds an atomic counter or gauge; at extreme worker counts (P > 256) the aggregate metrics-read cost at snapshot time grows linearly with worker count (mitigated: snapshot is pull-based and off the hot path, P8).
  • (-) Telemetry that nobody dashboards is dead weight; Operational Readiness's Dashboard Design section is the contract that every metric maps to a panel or is deliberately retired.

Operational implications. Any new invariant proposed after Phase 14 must add: a detection mechanism (assertion or comparison), a pcs-metrics counter/gauge, and a dashboard or alert threshold — mirroring the System Invariants table's columns. Reviewers reject an invariant-only PR that has no accompanying telemetry.

Violation consequence. Adding I3's ABA-prevention logic without a corresponding deque_top_wrap_near gauge (as very nearly happened during the initial design pass) would mean a wrap-around bug ships silently; the first sign of the defect would be a corrupted computation result in production with no diagnostic trail back to the deque.

7. System Invariants

ID Invariant Statement Why It Matters Enforced In Detection Telemetry Recovery Debugging
I1 A task is owned and executed by exactly one worker at a time; a won steal atomically transfers ownership Prevents double-execution, data corruption, and double-free of the task closure pcs-deque CAS in steal; TaskState transition Ready → Running via compare_exchange Duplicate TaskId in Running state in pcs-metrics spans StealStats::double_run_detected counter (should always be 0) CAS failure on state transition → task not executed by the loser; winner proceeds Enable feature = "debug-ownership": log TaskId + worker_id on every state transition; assert no TaskId appears twice in Running
I2 Chase-Lev: owner push/pop is wait-free; at most one thief wins any steal CAS Guarantees owner latency bound; prevents two workers from running the same task pcs-deque: pop uses store + fence + CAS only in the single-item contention case; steal uses CAS on top loom model-check in Phase 14 exhausts all interleavings steals_attempted vs steals_won ratio (≤ 1.0 always) Thief losing the CAS retries on next steal opportunity; no rollback needed Run loom test deque_concurrent_steal; check that steals_won ≤ steals_attempted across all runs
I3 No task is lost or executed twice; monotonic top counter defeats ABA Correctness of computation results; no silently dropped work pcs-deque: top is a monotonically increasing AtomicU64, not a wrapped index; steal checks top == expected after CAS win Assert tasks_submitted == tasks_completed at pool shutdown SchedMetrics::tasks_run vs tasks_submitted delta N/A — ABA avoidance is structural (never reset top) Run soak test: submit 10M tasks, assert counter equality at shutdown; also run with loom
I4 join(a, b) returns only after both a and b are Done; b may run on the calling thread Correctness of fork-join; caller's continuation may depend on both results pcs-parallel join: calling thread pops b from deque or steals it; waits via Latch if b runs elsewhere Assert both task states == Done before join returns join_latency_ns histogram in pcs-metrics N/A — join is blocking by contract; timeout API is not provided (use cancellation) Add debug_assert!(a.state == Done && b.state == Done) before join return; trace join entry/exit with TaskId
I5 A DAG node becomes Ready iff all its dependency edges are satisfied (pending_deps == 0) Correctness of task graph execution order; violation causes use-before-produce data races pcs-graph: GraphNode::notify_successor does fetch_sub(1); transition to Ready only on prev == 1 Cycle detection at graph construction (DFS); assert pending_deps never underflows (wraps past 0) graph_ready_events counter per node type Underflow detected via saturating subtract + assertion; log offending NodeId Print graph DOT dump on assertion failure; trace pending_deps value at each decrement
I6 Pool never has more than num_threads non-blocked workers running tasks (excluding compensation) Prevents oversubscription (P4); keeps cache footprint bounded pcs-pool: thread count is fixed at spawn; Phase 12 compensation threads are counted separately in compensation_active Assert active_workers ≤ num_threads in pool health check SchedMetrics::active_workers gauge; compensation_active gauge If assertion fires, log stack traces of all workers; compensation thread is temporary Track worker state machine (Idle / Running / Blocked / Compensating); periodically assert state histogram
I7 A panic in a task transitions it to Panicked; re-raises at the owning scope/join boundary Prevents silent data corruption from partial computation; upholds Rust's panic safety model pcs-core Task::run wraps closure in catch_unwind; sets TaskState::Panicked; Scope checks on task collect UnwindSafe bound on task closures; Scope::wait calls resume_unwind SchedMetrics::panicked_tasks counter Scope is poisoned on first child panic; remaining children are cancelled (Phase 11) Capture panic payload in Task; log TaskId + panic message before re-raise; backtrace available via RUST_BACKTRACE=1
I8 A Scope does not return until every task spawned within it has reached a terminal state Lifetime safety: scope borrows 's; if scope returned early, spawned tasks could access freed stack memory pcs-parallel Scope::drop / Scope::wait_all: spins executing tasks until internal Latch reaches 0 Rust borrow checker enforces 's at compile time; runtime latch count checked at scope exit scope_wait_ns histogram; scope_task_count histogram N/A — Scope drop blocks until all tasks done (by construction) Enable feature = "scope-trace": log scope enter/exit + task counts; assert latch == 0 at scope drop
I9 parallel_reduce result equals sequential left-fold for associative operators (up to documented float reassociation) Correctness of data-parallel computations; callers rely on numerical consistency pcs-parallel reduce: operator must be Fn(T, T) -> T + documented associative contract; float caveat in rustdoc Property-based tests in Phase 14: compare parallel reduce vs sequential for integer types (exact) N/A (result correctness, not a runtime metric) N/A — non-associative operators produce unspecified but deterministic results in single-thread mode Use deterministic mode (P9) to reproduce; compare with sequential reference; use integer reduce to isolate from float issues
I10 After shutdown, all worker threads are joined and no task remains Pending or Running Resource safety: no dangling threads; no orphaned task closures holding references pcs-pool ThreadPool::shutdown: sets shutdown flag, unparks all workers, calls JoinHandle::join for each; asserts deques empty Assert all TaskState ∈ {Done, Panicked, Cancelled} at shutdown completion tasks_pending_at_shutdown gauge (must be 0) If tasks remain after timeout, log task states + enqueue abort; escalate to process exit Run valgrind --leak-check=full on pool shutdown test; assert active_workers == 0 after join loop
I11 A CancellationToken is monotonic: once cancel() observes a transition to cancelled, it never reverts to not-cancelled Cooperative cancellation correctness; a flapping token would let a task alternately see cancelled/not-cancelled and behave nondeterministically pcs-sync CancellationToken::cancel: single SeqCst store from false; no API exists to clear the flag (ADR-12) Property test: after cancel(), is_cancelled() returns true on every subsequent poll from any clone cancellation_flips_detected counter (must stay 0; incremented only by a debug-assert canary in test builds) N/A — the type system provides no uncancel path; a violation would be a compiler/API-misuse bug, not a runtime condition Property-based test: interleave cancel() and is_cancelled() from N cloned tokens under loom; assert monotonicity across all interleavings
I12 Metrics and trace instrumentation never introduce a lock, CAS, or cross-thread synchronization onto the steal/pop/push hot path Upholds P8 (no global synchronization on the steal path); an observability feature that silently adds contention would defeat the scheduler's core scalability claim pcs-metrics: per-worker AtomicU64 with Relaxed ordering, no aggregation on the hot path; pcs-trace: per-worker SPSC ring buffer, no cross-thread write Benchmark parity check: steal_throughput micro-benchmark run with --features metrics,trace vs. without; assert < 2% delta hot_path_instrumentation_overhead_ns (measured periodically in CI perf-reg job, not in production) If a regression is found, the offending metric/span write is moved off the hot path (deferred to snapshot/flush time) or gated further behind sampling Run perf stat -e lock:contention on the deque push/pop/steal path with all features enabled; assert zero contention events attributable to pcs-metrics or pcs-trace

8. Domain Model

8.1 Core Entity Taxonomy

Entity Module Cardinality Owned By
Task pcs-core one per spawned closure ChaseLevDeque or Injector
TaskId pcs-core unique u64, monotonic pool-global counter
TaskState pcs-core per Task AtomicTaskState inside Task
ChaseLevDeque<Task> pcs-deque one per Worker Worker struct
Stealer<Task> pcs-deque N-1 clones per worker Worker.stealers[]
Worker pcs-worker one per thread ThreadPool
Injector<Task> pcs-pool one per pool (global) ThreadPool
ThreadPool pcs-pool one per process caller
GraphNode pcs-graph one per DAG task TaskGraph
Dependency pcs-graph one per DAG edge TaskGraph
Scope<'s> pcs-parallel stack-lifetime calling thread
Future<T> / Promise<T> pcs-sync paired 1:1 task result path
Latch / Barrier / WaitGroup pcs-sync shared refs joining parties
ParallelIterator / Producer pcs-parallel transient data-parallel call site
SchedMetrics / StealStats pcs-metrics one per worker Worker
NumaNode pcs-numa one per domain topology detection

8.2 TaskState Machine

        spawn()
  [external] ──────► Pending
                        │
         deps satisfied │ (or no deps)
                        ▼
               ┌──── Ready ◄───── pending_deps == 0 (DAG node)
               │        │
    stolen or  │  pop()  │ steal()
    own pop()  │         │
               └────────►Running
                            │
              ┌─────────────┼──────────────┐
          panic            Done         cancelled
              │             │               │
              ▼             ▼               ▼
          Panicked        Done           Cancelled

State-transition table (all transitions are CAS on AtomicTaskState; failed CAS = thief races, retry or drop):

From To Trigger Actor Invariant
Pending Ready deps decremented to 0 completing predecessor I5
Pending Ready no deps on spawn spawning thread —
Ready Running pop() or steal() wins owner or thief I1
Running Done closure returns executing worker I3
Running Panicked closure panics executing worker I7
Running Cancelled cancellation token checked executing worker I11
Panicked — terminal — I7 propagated at Scope
Done — terminal — I3

8.3 Chase-Lev Deque Memory Layout

  ChaseLevDeque<T>
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  bottom: AtomicIsize   (owner pushes here; wraps mod capacity) │
  │  top:    AtomicIsize   (thieves steal from here; monotonic)    │
  │  buf:    AtomicPtr<CircularBuf<T>>  (resized via new alloc)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  CircularBuf<T>
  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
  │  capacity: usize  (power of 2)   │
  │  data: [UnsafeCell<T>; capacity] │
  └──────────────────────────────────┘

Access semantics:

Operation Actor Order Returns Notes
push(t) owner Release on bottom () Grow if bottom-top >= capacity; allocate new buf, copy, swap ptr (Release), update bottom
pop() owner SeqCst on bottom decrement, Acquire-load top Option<T> LIFO; if bottom <= top after decrement, CAS top (last item race with thief)
steal() thief Acquire top, SeqCst CAS Option<T> FIFO; CAS top from observed to +1; fails if another thief wins or owner empties

ABA prevention: top is monotonically increasing (never reset); old top values cannot recur within a deque's lifetime.

Memory ordering matches Le et al. (PPoPP 2013): push uses Release on bottom; steal uses Acquire on top, SeqCst CAS; pop uses SeqCst on bottom to fence against concurrent steals.

8.4 Worker Loop State Machine

  ┌──────────────┐
  │  poll_local  │◄────────────────────────────────────┐
  └──────┬───────┘                                     │
         │ local deque empty                           │ task done
         ▼                                             │
  ┌──────────────┐  found task ┌──────────────┐        │
  │  try_steal   │────────────►│  run_task    │────────┘
  └──────┬───────┘             └──────────────┘
         │ all victims empty
         ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  check_injector      │─── found ──► push to local ──► poll_local
  └──────┬───────────────┘
         │ still empty
         ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  exponential_backoff │  (spin N yields, then park)
  └──────┬───────────────┘
         │ parked
         ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  parked (futex/condvar) │◄── unpark() on new task submission
  └──────────────────────┘

Victim selection order (SchedPolicy):

  1. LocalityAware: own-NUMA workers (shuffled by XorShift RNG), then cross-NUMA workers.
  2. WorkFirst: random uniform over all workers.
  3. HelpFirst: prioritize workers with deepest queues (sample two, pick max).

8.5 Fork-Join Execution Model

join(a, b) — work-first semantics:

  thread T:  push(b) → run(a)
               │            │
               │         a done → pop(b) [if still on deque] → run(b)
               │                 OR b stolen by T' → T runs other tasks
               │                                    → T waits via Latch
               ▼
             join returns after both Done

Scope<'s> lifetime contract:

  scope_enter: allocate Scope on stack; latch count = 0
     │
     ├─ spawn(f): latch.increment(); push Task(f) to local deque
     │
     └─ scope_exit: latch.wait()
          → executing worker runs other tasks while waiting (no idle block)
          → last task Done: latch.decrement_to_zero() → unblock scope owner

Panic capture: each task's closure wrapped in catch_unwind; panic payload stored in Task.panic_payload: Option<Box<dyn Any>>; re-raised at scope_exit after all tasks terminate (I7, I8).

8.6 Task DAG Model

EBNF for DAG construction:

graph      ::= 'TaskGraph::new()' { node_decl | edge_decl } 'graph.submit(pool)'
node_decl  ::= 'graph.add_node(task_fn)' '->' 'NodeId'
edge_decl  ::= 'graph.add_edge(pred: NodeId, succ: NodeId)'

Internal node state:

GraphNode {
  task:         Task,
  pending_deps: AtomicUsize,   // decremented by each completing predecessor
  successors:   Vec<NodeId>,   // notified on this node's completion
}

Readiness propagation (per-node on completion): for each successor in the completing node's successors list, the runtime performs an AcqRel fetch_sub(1) on that successor's pending_deps. Whichever decrement observes the return value 1 (meaning it drove the counter from 1 to 0 — the last outstanding dependency) is the sole decrement that pushes the successor's task to a worker deque, preferring the completing worker's own deque for locality (MM8). All other decrements return a value greater than 1 and take no further action, so the push happens exactly once with no lock or secondary check.

Cycle detection: topological sort at graph.submit(); reject if no ordering exists (return Err(CycleError)).

Completion latch: TaskGraph owns a Latch; the final node's completion fires it; graph.wait() participates in scheduling while waiting.

8.7 Data-Parallel Splitter Model

  • Producer<T>
    • len() -> usize
    • split_at(mid: usize) -> (Producer<T>, Producer<T>) // must be cheap O(1)
    • into_iter() -> impl Iterator<Item=T> // leaf execution

Recursive halving: the splitter compares the producer's remaining length against the granularity threshold. At or below threshold, it runs the leaf sequentially by handing the producer's iterator directly to the user closure f. Above threshold, it splits the producer at its midpoint into a left and right half and recurses on both halves through join — the left half runs on the spawning worker (work-first, ADR-21) while the right half is exposed on the deque for stealing. Recursion bottoms out only when every leaf has reached the threshold, so the tree depth is O(log(total_len / threshold)).

Granularity threshold: max(MIN_GRAIN, total_len / (num_threads * OVERDECOMPOSE_FACTOR)); default OVERDECOMPOSE_FACTOR = 8 (P6).

Adaptive splitting: if steal pressure detected (steal_ratio > 0.5 in last epoch), lower threshold by 25%; if low pressure, raise by 10% (bounded by [MIN_GRAIN, MAX_GRAIN]).

Primitives and associativity requirements:

Primitive Combiner constraint Output
parallel_for none (side-effectful) ()
parallel_map none Vec<B>
parallel_reduce associative, identity required T
parallel_scan associative, identity required Vec<T> (prefix sums)

I9: parallel_reduce result equals sequential left-fold for associative operators; float reassociation documented as non-default.

8.8 Future / Promise Ownership Model

Promise<T>  ──write-once──►  inner: Arc<PromiseInner<T>>
Future<T>   ──read──────────►  inner: Arc<PromiseInner<T>>

PromiseInner<T> {
    value:   UnsafeCell<Option<T>>,
    state:   AtomicU8,          // 0=Pending, 1=Fulfilled, 2=Panicked
    waiter:  AtomicPtr<Latch>,  // registered by Future::wait()
    conts:   Mutex<Vec<Task>>,  // continuation tasks chained post-fulfill
}

Future::wait() contract: does not block the OS thread; instead, the waiting worker runs other tasks from its deque until the PromiseInner.state transitions to Fulfilled/Panicked (spin + park via the embedded Latch).

Continuation chaining: future.then(|val| ...) registers a Task in conts; on fulfillment all conts are pushed to the fulfilling worker's deque (locality).

8.9 Sync Primitives Semantics

Primitive Count direction Release trigger Wait behavior
Latch decrement to 0 count == 0 participating (runs tasks)
Barrier increment to N all N arrive participating
WaitGroup decrement to 0 count == 0 participating

"Participating wait": the blocking worker does not park the OS thread; it polls its local deque and attempts steals until the condition is satisfied.

8.10 NUMA Domain Model

NumaNode {
    id:        u32,
    workers:   Vec<WorkerId>,
    injector:  Injector<Task>,    // per-domain overflow queue
    memory:    MemoryBinding,     // OS NUMA memory policy handle
}

Steal order (I9 in context of Phase 9):

  1. Own worker's deque (local pop).
  2. Other workers in same NumaNode (local steal).
  3. Per-domain Injector (global domain queue).
  4. Workers in other NumaNodes (remote steal — last resort).

Memory binding: TaskData allocated via NumaAllocator that calls mbind/set_mempolicy on Linux to pin data pages to the local NUMA node.

8.11 Scheduling Metrics Layout (Storage / Wire)

Per-worker atomic counters (no lock; read by sampling thread for aggregation):

Field Type Semantics
tasks_run AtomicU64 total tasks executed by this worker
steals_attempted AtomicU64 steal attempts (including failed)
steals_won AtomicU64 successful steals
park_count AtomicU64 times worker parked
idle_ns AtomicU64 cumulative nanoseconds parked
deque_depth_max AtomicUsize high-water mark of local deque depth
task_latency_sum_ns AtomicU64 sum of (done_time - ready_time)
task_latency_count AtomicU64 sample count for latency avg

Export format (pcs-metrics → pcs-trace):

MetricsSnapshot {
    timestamp_ns: u64,
    workers: Vec<WorkerSnapshot>,    // per-worker counters
    pool_injector_depth: usize,
    numa_steal_cross: u64,
}

Serialized as newline-delimited JSON for prometheus scrape or file export; binary format: little-endian packed struct for low-overhead sampling.

8.12 Trace Event Layout (pcs-trace)

Event types (stored in a ring buffer per worker; exported on demand):

Event Fields Trigger
TaskSpawn task_id, parent_id, worker_id, ts_ns spawn / push
TaskStart task_id, worker_id, ts_ns pop() or steal() → Running
TaskDone task_id, worker_id, ts_ns, duration_ns closure returns
TaskStolen task_id, thief_id, victim_id, ts_ns steal() CAS wins
TaskPanicked task_id, worker_id, ts_ns catch_unwind catches
WorkerParked worker_id, ts_ns park() call
WorkerUnparked worker_id, ts_ns, trigger_task_id unpark() call
ScopeEnter scope_id, worker_id, ts_ns Scope created
ScopeExit scope_id, worker_id, ts_ns, task_count Scope drops

Flamegraph export: SpawnTree reconstructed from (parent_id → child_ids) edges in TaskSpawn events; rendered as Brendan Gregg folded-stack format for inferno-flamegraph.

Ring buffer: lock-free SPSC (one producer = owning worker, one consumer = trace export thread); capacity configurable (default 65536 events); overflow = oldest event dropped + drop counter incremented.

8.13 Worked Example — Fork-Join Parallel Mergesort

The caller opens one scope (pool.spawn_scope) and spawns a single top-level sort task covering the whole array. That task's recursive step checks its own span against GRAIN: at or below the threshold it falls back to a sequential insertion sort and returns immediately; above threshold it computes the midpoint and issues one join of two recursive halves. Per the work-first policy (ADR-21), the left half's continuation is pushed to the local deque while the right half runs inline on the calling worker — so the pushed half is the one available for another worker to steal. After both halves of the join are Done, the task merges the two sorted halves in place. Every recursive call repeats this same check-split-join-merge pattern until all spans are at or below GRAIN.

Task tree (4-element, GRAIN=1, 2 workers W0/W1):

  W0 spawns split(0,4): pushes right(2,4), runs left(0,2)
    W1 steals right(2,4): splits → pushes (3,4), runs (2,3)
    W0 after left(0,2) done: pops (1,2) or steals if W1 took it
  → 4 leaf tasks, 2 internal merges per side, 1 final merge
  → W0 and W1 share work; no central lock

8.14 Failure-Path Example — DAG with Panicking Node

  graph:  A → B → D
              ↓
          C → D

  A panics:
    1. A.state CAS: Running → Panicked
    2. A's successors (B, C): pending_deps.fetch_sub(1) → B.pending = 0, C.pending = 0
    3. B and C become Ready; both execute
    4. B.run() → B detects A panicked via shared PanicFlag → B transitions to Panicked (propagation policy)
    5. D's pending decrements from both B and C; if policy=abort-on-first-panic, D is never pushed
    6. graph.wait() returns Err(PropagatedPanic { origin: A.id, payload: ... })

Alternative policy (best-effort): run B, C, D regardless; collect all panics; return Err(MultiPanic(vec)).

8.15 Edge Cases and Pitfalls

Scenario Risk Mitigation
Single-item deque: owner pop races with steal Lost task or double-run CAS on top by owner in pop() last-item path; loser retries or returns None
Buffer growth during steal Thief reads stale buffer pointer Buffer swap uses Release store; thief does Acquire load before reading slot
join(a,b) — b stolen before a finishes Owner has no work; may park Owner's park wakes on any unpark, including b's completion latch
Scope with 0 spawned tasks latch.wait() blocks forever Latch initialized to 0 → wait() returns immediately
DAG with 0-dep nodes all submitted at once Thundering herd on injector Distribute across per-NUMA injectors round-robin
parallel_scan with non-associative op Wrong prefix sums Static assertion / trait bound requiring Associative marker trait
Cancellation mid-steal Task stolen after cancel issued Cancellation token checked at task start; late steal sees token, drops task
All workers blocking in Future::wait() Pool deadlock Compensation thread injection (Phase 12); bounded at 2 × num_threads
TaskId overflow (u64 wrap) Duplicate IDs in trace Saturating counter + pool restart required (documented; ~10^19 tasks)
Scope panic with in-flight tasks Resources not freed scope_exit still waits (I8); all tasks reach terminal state before propagating

9. Tech Stack ADR Table

ADR # Decision Locked Choice Rejected Alternatives Rationale When the Rejected Choice Wins
ADR-01 Implementation language Rust (2021 edition) C++, Go, Zig Memory safety without GC; unsafe scoped to deque hot path; ownership = structured concurrency; Send/Sync enforced at compile time C++ wins in embedded/RTOS contexts with no Rust toolchain; Go wins when goroutines and GC-pause tolerance are acceptable trade-offs for developer velocity; Zig wins for freestanding environments with no stdlib
ADR-02 Build system Cargo workspace; pcs-* crates in [workspace.members] Bazel, Buck2, CMake Cargo is the Rust standard; workspace deduplicates deps; cargo bench, cargo test native Bazel wins in monorepos mixing Rust, C++, and Python needing hermetic, remote-cache builds; Buck2 wins at Meta-scale polyglot repositories; CMake wins when the scheduler must be vendored into a legacy C++ project
ADR-03 MSRV (minimum supported Rust version) Rust 1.80 (locked) latest-only, nightly AtomicUsize::fetch_update stable; std::hint::spin_loop stable; avoids nightly breakage Latest-only wins when the project is internal to one team and builds nightly; nightly wins if using unstable features (const generics for lane counts, asm! for platform-specific fences) that meaningfully reduce unsafe surface
ADR-04 Dependency policy Minimal; no async runtime dep; no tokio/async-std pulling rayon as a dep pcs-core is dependency-free; pcs-deque has zero deps; only dev-deps for test/bench Pulling rayon wins when the primary goal is a production application (not a learning exercise) and time-to-first-speedup matters more than deep understanding; also wins when the organization has already standardized on rayon
ADR-05 Chase-Lev deque implementation Custom pcs-deque (from scratch per project goal) crossbeam-deque, deque crate Project mandate; implement C11 orderings per Le et al.; full control over growth policy crossbeam-deque wins in any production system where audit risk of novel lock-free code outweighs learning value; it is the battle-tested choice for shipping software
ADR-06 MPMC global injector crossbeam-queue::SegQueue (or custom if dep policy allows) std::sync::Mutex<VecDeque>, lock-free ring SegQueue is MPMC lock-free; acceptable external dep; alternative = custom if zero-dep mandate extends Mutex<VecDeque> wins when num_threads ≤ 4 and lock contention is absent; a lock-free ring buffer wins when the producer count is bounded and CAS contention must be further reduced via FAA (see P-M, Yang & Mellor-Crummey)
ADR-07 Thread parking mechanism std::thread::park / unpark + AtomicBool flag futex syscall direct, condvar, parking_lot::park park/unpark is safe, portable, sufficient latency; parking_lot adds a dep; condvar has unnecessary mutex Direct futex wins on Linux when park/unpark latency must be < 2 µs and portability is irrelevant; parking_lot wins when fine-grained lock-with-timeout semantics are needed (e.g., timed join deadlock detection)
ADR-08 RNG for victim selection XorShift32 inline (no dep) rand crate, SmallRng Victim selection needs fast, reproducible, per-worker RNG; XorShift is 3 ops, no allocation, no syscall rand::SmallRng (ChaCha8) wins when security properties are needed (randomized scheduling to prevent adversarial timing attacks) or when statistical quality matters more than raw speed
ADR-09 Hardware topology detection hwloc crate (optional) + /sys/devices/system/cpu fallback num_cpus, affinity crate hwloc gives NUMA distances and CpuSet; /sys fallback for environments without hwloc; optional dep gated by feature flag num_cpus wins in simple single-socket deployments where NUMA topology never varies and the binary must be minimal; affinity crate wins when targeting Windows and macOS portability is required
ADR-10 CPU affinity binding libc::sched_setaffinity on Linux; thread_affinity crate on macOS/Windows OS-provided thread pools Direct sched_setaffinity = lowest latency; cross-platform abstraction crate acceptable for portability OS-provided thread pools win in containerized cloud environments where the scheduler inside the pool is already NUMA-aware and affinity binding is handled externally (e.g., Kubernetes CPU manager)
ADR-11 Panic handling in tasks std::panic::catch_unwind per task closure process abort, #[no_unwind] catch_unwind preserves task DAG integrity; panics stored and re-raised at scope boundary (I7) Process abort (panic = "abort" in Cargo.toml) wins in safety-critical or embedded contexts where partial results from surviving siblings are worse than a clean crash; wins when the binary size savings from removing unwind tables matter
ADR-12 Cancellation model Cooperative: CancellationToken (Arc<AtomicBool>) polled at task checkpoints Preemptive signal-based, pthread_cancel Preemptive unsafe in Rust; cooperative is composable; tasks opt-in to checking token Preemptive cancellation wins in real-time systems where worst-case task duration must be bounded and task authors cannot be trusted to poll a token; signal-based wins for legacy C FFI tasks that have natural signal-handler cancellation paths
ADR-13 Serialization for metrics/trace export serde_json (newline-delimited) + raw little-endian struct for binary protobuf, MessagePack, CBOR JSON readable for debugging; binary struct for low-overhead continuous sampling; no schema dep Protobuf wins in multi-language environments where the trace consumer is Go/Python/Java and schema evolution with backward compatibility is a hard requirement; MessagePack wins for embedded targets where JSON is too verbose
ADR-14 Metrics collection Per-worker AtomicU64 counters; snapshot-pull model prometheus crate push, metrics facade Zero contention on hot path; pull-on-demand avoids periodic overhead; prometheus acceptable for pcs-metrics feature gate prometheus crate wins in cloud deployments where Prometheus scraping is already the organizational standard and push-pull configuration is operationally simpler than embedding a pull endpoint; metrics facade wins in library code that must be agnostic to backend
ADR-15 Task tracing / flamegraph Per-worker SPSC ring buffer of TraceEvent; inferno for flamegraph rendering tracing crate integration, perf tracing crate adds async assumptions; ring buffer is scheduler-native; inferno dev-dep only tracing crate wins in projects already using the Tokio ecosystem where structured spans and async-aware context propagation matter; perf wins for kernel-level profiling of syscall and hardware counter attribution
ADR-16 Testing framework cargo test (unit + integration); proptest for property tests on deque quickcheck, custom harness proptest shrinking superior for concurrent data structure invariants; standard cargo test for integration quickcheck wins in teams already familiar with Haskell-style arbitrary instances and preferring a simpler API; a custom harness wins when the concurrent test harness needs deterministic seed injection tied to loom thread scheduling
ADR-17 Concurrency model checker loom for deque and sync primitives MIRI (memory not concurrency), shuttle loom is the Rust standard for model-checking lock-free code; permutes orderings; catches deque ABA and ordering bugs MIRI wins for detecting memory-safety UB and use-after-free that loom does not model (loom only checks concurrency); shuttle wins for randomized concurrency testing when exhaustive exploration is too expensive (> 3 concurrent actors)
ADR-18 Fuzzing cargo-fuzz (libFuzzer) on task graph construction and deque operations afl.rs, honggfuzz cargo-fuzz is default Rust fuzzing; no extra toolchain; fuzz DAG cycle detection, deque growth, cancellation paths honggfuzz wins for hardware-feedback-guided fuzzing (Intel PT) that explores deeper code paths than coverage-only fuzzing; afl.rs wins in CI environments with AFL++ toolchain already deployed
ADR-19 Benchmarking criterion crate; benches in pcs-cli std::time, hyperfine, divan criterion statistical rigor; warmup; reports speedup curves; pcs-cli runs parallel mergesort, n-body, BFS, matmul divan wins for very low-overhead micro-benchmarks (no warmup iterations, compile-time benchmark registration); hyperfine wins for end-to-end CLI comparison where process startup time must be included
ADR-20 Determinism / replay mode Single-thread executor in SchedPolicy::Deterministic; fixed task order none, external record-replay Required for debugging nondeterministic bugs; single-thread mode exercises same task logic without scheduling noise (P9) External record-replay wins in distributed systems where multiple nodes must be replayed together (the local single-thread mode cannot capture inter-node interactions)
ADR-21 Fork-join continuation style Work-first (push continuation, run child inline) as default; HelpFirst as config Help-first default, continuation stealing Work-first maximizes cache locality for divide-and-conquer; empirically superior for CPU-bound tasks (Cilk research) Help-first wins for producer-consumer pipelines (Phase 5 DAG stages) where the parent continuation must run immediately to unblock downstream consumers, and stack depth is a concern; also wins for breadth-first search where frontier expansion must be wide
ADR-22 NUMA memory allocation libnuma via libc bindings; mbind/set_mempolicy on Linux jemalloc NUMA, mimalloc Direct libnuma gives precise node binding; jemalloc NUMA support is heuristic; optional via numa feature flag jemalloc NUMA wins when the allocator is already jemalloc and fine-grained mbind per allocation is too expensive; mimalloc wins for Windows NUMA workloads where libnuma is unavailable
ADR-23 CI/CD GitHub Actions; matrix: [stable, MSRV] × [linux-x86_64, linux-aarch64]; loom pass; cargo-fuzz nightly job GitLab CI, CircleCI GitHub Actions native for open-source Rust; loom requires dedicated job with RUSTFLAGS="--cfg loom"; fuzz on nightly GitLab CI wins in self-hosted enterprise environments where GitHub access is restricted; CircleCI wins for organizations already standardized on its resource-class system for NUMA-hardware test nodes
ADR-24 Licensing MIT OR Apache-2.0 (dual) GPL, BSL, proprietary Matches Rust ecosystem convention; permissive for library use; dual license maximizes compatibility GPL wins when the scheduler is intended as a standalone tool (not embedded library) and copyleft enforcement is organizationally mandated; BSL wins for commercial source-available products with a future open-source conversion commitment
ADR-25 Reproducibility Cargo.lock committed; [workspace.resolver] = "2"; pinned CI toolchain via rust-toolchain.toml floating MSRV, no lockfile Locked deps prevent supply-chain drift; rust-toolchain.toml pins exact nightly for fuzz job Floating MSRV wins for library crates published to crates.io where consumers should pin their own lock files; a rolling-latest policy wins for internal tools where always-latest-stable is the deployment model
ADR-26 Feature flags numa, metrics, trace, loom (test-only), deterministic always-on, runtime flags Zero-cost when disabled; loom flag replaces std::sync atomics with loom stubs; numa gates hwloc dep Always-on wins for internal tools where binary size is irrelevant and every feature needs to be observable in production; runtime flags win when feature toggling must survive a process restart without recompile (e.g., dynamic tracing enable/disable via sysctl)
ADR-27 Blocking task compensation Spawn replacement thread up to 2 × num_threads cap; BlockingTask::spawn(f) API fixed thread count only, rayon::spawn_blocking analogue Prevents deadlock when all workers block in Future::wait() inside join (Phase 12, P7); bounded cap prevents oversubscription Fixed thread count wins in hard-real-time systems where the OS thread count must be known statically at startup; rayon::spawn_blocking analogue wins when the application already uses Tokio/async-std and blocking bridge utilities are provided by the async runtime
ADR-28 Documentation rustdoc with doc-tests for public API; ARCHITECTURE.md in repo root with canonical diagram mdBook, external wiki rustdoc co-located with code; doc-tests exercise public signatures; ARCHITECTURE.md references section numbers from this blueprint mdBook wins for large documentation sites with cross-referencing, search, and multiple render targets (PDF, ePub) needed by non-developer stakeholders; an external wiki wins when documentation is written by a dedicated technical-writing team separate from the code
