Performance Engineering

Benchmarking Strategy

All benchmarks live in pcs-cli/benches/; run with cargo bench using criterion.

Benchmark Parameters swept Metric
steal_throughput threads: 1,2,4,8,16,32; tasks: 10k,100k tasks/sec; steal rate
parallel_mergesort threads: 1..N; array: 1M,64M elements wall time; speedup vs N
fork_join_fibonacci threads: 1..N; N=38 wall time; efficiency
parallel_reduce_sum threads: 1..N; elements: 10M throughput (elem/sec)
dag_linear_chain chain length: 100,1000,10k; threads: 4,8 latency per node
dag_diamond_wide fan-out: 10,100,1000; threads: 4,8 total latency
scope_spawn_overhead spawn count: 1,10,100,1000 per scope ns/spawn
idle_steal_loop all workers idle; one task injected wakeup latency (ns)
deque_push_pop single-thread microbench ns/op
deque_steal_contention 1 owner + N-1 stealers; N=2..16 steal success rate; ns/steal
n_body_parallel threads: 1..N; bodies: 10k sim steps/sec
bfs_parallel threads: 1..N; graph: 1M nodes edges/sec
matrix_multiply threads: 1..N; 1024x1024 f64 GFLOP/s

Throughput and Latency Targets (tie to Constraints, Phase 14)

Each target below carries a stable M# ID so it can be cited elsewhere (Hard Questions, Capacity Model) without restating the value.

M# Target Value Condition
M1 Linear speedup efficiency ≥ 80% at 8 threads parallel_mergesort, 64 M elements
M2 Steal overhead fraction < 5% of total task time measured as steals_won * steal_cost_ns / total_ns
M3 Task spawn latency (scope) < 150 ns median scope_spawn_overhead, N=1
M4 Idle wakeup latency < 5 µs p99 idle_steal_loop
M5 Deque push/pop (owner) < 10 ns single-thread microbench
M6 Steal operation (uncontended) < 30 ns 2-thread harness
M7 Memory per in-flight task < 256 bytes amortized measured under peak load
M8 RSS growth per 1 M tasks < 10 MB soak test
M9 Speedup efficiency at 16 threads ≥ 65% parallel_mergesort, 64 M elements (matches §4 benchmark-scaling-efficiency row, bandwidth-limited per Capacity Model step 5)
M10 NUMA cross-domain steal penalty ≤ 3× same-domain steal cost numa_comp_affinity-style cross-socket steal benchmark (Phase 9)

Profiling Methodology

profiling workflow:
  1. cargo bench --bench <target> -- --profile-time 30
  2. perf record -g -F 997 -- ./target/release/pcs-cli bench <workload>
  3. perf script | inferno-collapse-perf | inferno-flamegraph > flame.svg
  4. inspect: steal loop hot paths; atomic CAS retry counts; park/unpark syscall fraction
  5. for NUMA: numastat + perf mem record; check cross-domain accesses
  • On macOS: cargo instruments --template "Time Profiler" via cargo-instruments.
  • pcs-trace low-overhead sampling (Phase 13): enable with --features trace; export Chromium JSON or Flamescope format.
  • Heap profiler: jemalloc with jemallocator crate; jemalloc_ctl for epoch stats; MALLOC_CONF=prof:true for heap profiles.

Flamegraph Workflow (pcs-trace integration)

  • pcs-trace records per-task (TaskId, worker_id, start_ns, end_ns, parent_id) in a lock-free ring buffer.
  • Export: pcs_trace::export_flamegraph(path) collapses by task tree depth; outputs folded format compatible with inferno.
  • CI artifact: flamegraph SVG generated for parallel_mergesort nightly; diff against previous run flagged if any frame shifts > 10%.

Lock / Contention Analysis

Path Expected contention Detection
owner push/pop zero (wait-free) perf stat -e lock:contention should show 0 on owner thread
steal CAS moderate under imbalance StealStats::steals_attempted / steals_won ratio; target > 0.5
Injector enqueue/dequeue low (only overflow + external) lock-free MPMC; perf stat -e cache-misses on injector cache line
Latch / Barrier futex only at join points strace -e futex counts; target: O(scope_count), not O(task_count)
TaskState CAS per-task, uncontended single compare-exchange per transition; no retry loop

Cache Analysis

  • False sharing: ChaseLevDeque bottom and top atomics on separate cache lines (#[repr(align(64))]).
  • Worker struct: Worker padded to 64-byte boundary; each worker's hot fields (deque bottom, rng state) on own line.
  • Stolen task locality: FIFO steal takes oldest task (coarsest granularity, P3); child task is cache-cold by design; benefit: less data in thief's cache to evict.
  • NUMA locality (Phase 9): allocate task payload on initiating NUMA node; pcs-numa uses mbind/set_mempolicy; validate with numastat.
  • L1/L2 reuse: pcs-metrics tracks cache_misses_per_task via perf_event on Linux (optional feature perf-events).

Allocator Analysis

  • Default: system allocator; optional jemallocator feature for production profiling.
  • Task allocation: Box<dyn FnOnce() + Send> — one heap alloc per spawn; target: amortized reuse via slab if alloc dominates.
  • Deque buffer: exponential growth (double on overflow); tracked via pcs-metrics::alloc_bytes.
  • Allocation profiling: heaptrack or cargo-valgrind --tool massif; assert RSS plateau in soak.

Syscall Analysis

Syscall Source Target
futex(WAIT/WAKE) park/unpark workers O(1) per idle transition; not per task
mmap deque buffer growth < 1 per 10k tasks (amortized)
mbind / set_mempolicy NUMA allocation once per task batch, not per task
sched_setaffinity CpuSet binding once per worker thread at startup
clock_gettime pcs-metrics timestamps gated behind --features metrics; VDSO path (no syscall)

Scalability Analysis

Dominant Scaling Pressures

Pressure Source Onset
Steal contention multiple thieves competing for same victim's top visible at N > 16 with fine-grained tasks
False sharing on injector MPMC head/tail on same cache line measurable at N > 8 external submitters
NUMA cross-domain traffic steals crossing NUMA boundary visible at N > physical core count per socket
DAG completion fan-out O(successors) wakeups per node completion large fan-out (>1000) creates wakeup storms
Scope join overhead Latch decrement + futex wake per task O(task_count) decrement; futex only at final join
TaskId counter contention global AtomicU64 fetch_add negligible; single atomic on spawn path
Trace ring buffer lock-free bounded ring; drops on overflow tracing overhead O(tasks_run); disable in production

Scaling Ceilings

Ceiling Cause Mitigation
Amdahl sequential fraction task graph critical path; data dependencies profile critical path depth; increase parallelism
Memory bandwidth saturation parallel reduce over large arrays locality-aware splitting (P3); NUMA-local allocation (Phase 9)
Work imbalance (straggler) uneven task granularity steal-half option (Phase 7); adaptive splitting (Phase 6)
Over-decomposition too many tiny tasks; steal cost > task cost granularity threshold (P6); pcs-sched auto-tuner (Phase 7)
OS thread count num_threads ≤ physical cores (P4) no mitigation — by design; blocking tasks use compensation (Phase 12)
Deque buffer churn rapid grow/shrink exponential growth only; no shrink; bounded by max task depth

Vertical Scaling (single machine, more cores)

Thread count Expected behavior
1 sequential; steal path never taken; deterministic mode equivalent
2–8 near-linear speedup for compute-bound; steal overhead < 5%
8–32 efficiency degrades ~10–15% due to cross-socket NUMA; Phase 9 mitigates
32–64 L3 contention visible; NUMA-local injectors critical; steal-half reduces traffic
> 64 global Injector becomes bottleneck; per-domain injectors (Phase 9) required

Horizontal Scaling

  • This scheduler is a single-process, single-machine component; horizontal scaling is out of scope at the pool level.
  • Multi-node distribution: out of scope; DAG interface (pcs-graph) is designed for single-address-space.
  • Embedding in a distributed system: pool acts as a local executor; inter-node coordination is the caller's responsibility.

Coordination Overhead Analysis

Operation Coordination cost Scales as
Spawn into local deque 0 synchronization (wait-free push) O(1)
Steal from victim 1 CAS on victim's top O(1) per steal
DAG node completion 1 fetch_sub per dependency edge O(in-degree) per node
Scope join 1 fetch_sub per task + 1 futex at zero O(scope_size), futex O(1)
Barrier 1 fetch_add per arrival + 1 futex broadcast O(participants)
Shutdown park-all signal + N thread joins O(num_threads)

Task Graph Scaling

Metric Behavior
Node count (DAG) O(N) memory in pcs-graph; GraphNode ~64 bytes; 1M nodes ≈ 64 MB
Edge count O(E) adjacency storage; sparse graphs preferred
Ready-set propagation O(out-degree) per completion; broadcast storms at high fan-out
DAG submit latency O(V + E) cycle check at submission; acceptable for graphs up to ~100k nodes
Incremental DAG not supported in Phase 5 baseline; future extension point

Deque and Injector Growth

Structure Growth policy Bound
ChaseLevDeque buffer double on overflow bounded by max recursion depth × task size; typically < 1 MB per worker
Injector<Task> unbounded MPMC; backpressure via P10 submit() blocks when in-flight > PoolConfig::max_in_flight
TaskGraph node store pre-allocated slab; resizes on batch submit linear in node count; no per-edge allocation beyond Vec
Trace ring buffer fixed-size circular; drops on overflow configurable TRACE_RING_CAPACITY; default 1M entries × 40 bytes = 40 MB

Scheduling Metadata Scaling

  • StealStats: per-worker atomics; O(num_threads) total; no contention between workers.
  • SchedMetrics histograms: fixed-bucket HDR histograms; O(1) record; O(1) snapshot; memory O(buckets × num_threads).
  • pcs-trace span store: bounded ring buffer; overflow drops oldest spans; no unbounded growth.
  • Worker stealers array: Box<[Stealer<Task>]> of length N-1 per worker; O(N²) total Stealer handles; negligible for N ≤ 256.

Known Scaling Limits and Thresholds

Limit Value Notes
Max practical thread count 256 Stealer matrix is O(N²); above 256 use per-domain groups
Max DAG nodes per submit ~1M cycle-check is O(V+E); beyond this, partition into sub-graphs
Max steal depth before park configurable; default 8 × N steal attempts exponential backoff before futex park
Max tasks in-flight PoolConfig::max_in_flight; default 64 × num_threads backpressure gate at Injector
Granularity floor task body must exceed steal cost by 10× (P6) auto-tuner (Phase 7) enforces at runtime

Mental Models & First Principles

Each model is a compressed law of the scheduler's behavior. Violating one of these leads to a well-defined bug class.

MM1 — Idle cores pull; busy cores never push. Work-stealing is a demand-driven protocol. The thief seeks work; the owner never donates. Prediction: contention scales with steal rate, not with task count. When you see lock contention on top, the problem is task granularity (tasks too small → too many steals), not thread count. Applied: §8.3 (CAS on top), Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model.

MM2 — LIFO pop; FIFO steal — two different schedulers share one deque. The owner pops LIFO (hot cache, depth-first expansion of the most recent fork). The thief steals FIFO (the oldest, coarsest task with the most remaining work per steal). These policies are not contradictory; they are complementary. Bug without it: writing a FIFO pop path "for fairness" destroys cache locality and breaks the B&L makespan bound. Applied: ADR-01, Phase 2, P-A.

MM3 — T∞ is the wall-clock floor; T1 sets the ceiling on speedup. Blumofe-Leiserson: E[time] ≤ T1/P + O(T∞). No parallelism strategy can beat T∞; no more than P processors help beyond T1/T∞ (parallelism). Prediction: doubling threads past the parallelism limit yields zero speedup. Applied: Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model, §4 System Constraints, Phase 1 sizing, P-B.

MM4 — Memory orderings are per-operation contracts, not per-object settings. A Relaxed load on bottom is correct only because the subsequent Acquire load on top establishes the happens-before edge that makes the load safe. Changing either without analyzing the full edge is a data race. Bug without it: downgrading one ordering in isolation causes intermittent steal-of-already-popped-slot. Applied: §8.3 memory-ordering table, Phase 2, P-C.

MM5 — A deque that never shrinks avoids the ABA problem on buffer reuse. The Chase-Lev buffer grows (doubles) but never shrinks. Old buffers are kept alive until no thief can hold a pointer to them (epoch guard). If you free eagerly, a thief reads a recycled slot and sees a new, unrelated task. Applied: §8.3, I3, Phase 2.

MM6 — Work-first continuation placement is a form of DFS; help-first is BFS. Work-first (spawner runs child, thief steals continuation) → depth-first traversal → linear stack space in the number of critical path nodes. Help-first → breadth-first → O(leaves) stack frames simultaneously alive. Prediction: work-first uses less memory for balanced trees; help-first fills pipeline stages faster. Applied: ADR-21, Phase 4, P-D, P-F.

MM7 — Acquire/Release pairing is symmetric: every Release write needs an Acquire read. SeqCst is not "just being safe." It is a global total order, which is more expensive than necessary for the deque's producer-consumer boundary. The exact Release/Acquire pair suffices. Bug without it: using Relaxed on the Release side lets the compiler reorder store-to-slot after the bottom increment; thief reads garbage. Applied: P-C, §8.3.

MM8 — pending_deps is a countdown, not an allow-list. In DAG scheduling, pending_deps is decremented (not checked against a list) on each predecessor completion. The task becomes ready when it hits 0. Prediction: there is no "predecessor set scan" at completion — just one fetch_sub and a conditional push. Bug without it: decrement before the slot is visible → double-decrement → ready task submitted twice. Applied: §8.6, Phase 5, I5.

MM9 — Park/unpark is a level-triggered mechanism, not edge-triggered. thread::park() returns if an unpark was already issued (it drains a token). Spinning before parking burns CPU; parking too early misses the token. The correct protocol: check work, issue a few random steal attempts, check again, then park. Prediction: a single spurious unpark per sleep cycle is harmless. Bug without it: worker parks with work on global injector → deadlock until next external submit. Applied: Phase 3, ADR-07, B9.

MM10 — Compensation threads cure blocking, not overload. A compensation thread is injected when a worker thread calls block_in_place. It is not spawned to add parallelism under load; it is spawned to prevent starvation when a worker is stuck in blocking I/O or a mutex. Prediction: bounding compensation threads to K × num_threads prevents thread exhaustion. Bug without it: all workers block in user code → no one drains injector → live-lock until OS kills process. Applied: Phase 12, ADR-27.

Comparative Architecture Table

This scheduler (PCS) is a single-process, shared-memory, work-stealing fork-join runtime. The table below compares it against five production systems across six scheduling axes.

Axis PCS (this project) Cilk-5 / OpenCilk (P-F) Intel TBB (P-G) Java ForkJoinPool (P-H) Rayon (R1/R8) Go runtime goroutine scheduler (R5)
Task granularity model Fork-join + DAG; explicit Scope<'s>; adaptive split threshold (pcs-sched) Cilk keyword spawn/sync; compiler transforms; runtime granularity hint grain task_group / parallel_for with partitioner; adaptive grain auto-tuning ForkJoinTask / RecursiveTask; compute() override ParallelIterator via split(); user-controlled min_len/max_len Goroutines; no explicit grain; runtime multiplexes on M threads
Scheduling discipline Work-first by default; configurable HelpFirst/LocalityAware (ADR-21) Work-first by default (the paper that named the policy, P-F) Help-first (continue_task); task graph bypass for task_group Work-first inside fork(); help-first inside invoke() Work-first; continuation stolen M:N cooperative; preemptive at async points; sysmon preempts long G
Dependency / DAG model First-class TaskGraph with typed edges; cycle-reject at submit Implicit via structured spawn/sync; no named edges task_group flow graph with make_edge; explicit predecessor nodes CountedCompleter countdown pattern No first-class DAG; compose via join()/rayon::join No DAG primitives; use channels + sync
Locality awareness Per-worker Chase-Lev deque; NUMA-domain injector groups (Phase 9); within-domain steal bias Per-worker deque; no explicit NUMA support Arena-per-NUMA-node; task_arena::attach; allocator pinning No NUMA; JVM GC moves objects between pools No explicit NUMA; OS decides thread placement procPin per-P affinity; no NUMA API
Load balancing FIFO steal; steal-half variant (Phase 7); random victim selection with NUMA bias FIFO steal; random victim; provably efficient (P-B bound) Randomized stealing from tail of victim's deque Randomized; FIFO steal; async steal mode Randomized; FIFO steal; breadth-first on small work items Work-stealing across Ps; sysmon distributes goroutines
Fault / panic handling Scope poisoning; catch_unwind + re-raise at join boundary (S8); structured cleanup longjmp-based abort; no structured cleanup pre-OpenCilk Exception propagates to task_group::wait(); non-cancellable path RecursiveAction propagates via completeExceptionally Panic propagates to rayon::join; caught via catch_unwind Goroutine panic propagates to recover() in deferred calls

Per-system paragraph

Cilk-5 / OpenCilk is the academic ancestor of every work-first scheduler. The PCS design follows its theoretical model exactly (P-B T1/P + T∞ bound; P-F work-first continuation placement) but adds explicit NUMA locality, a DAG layer, and a typed Rust API.

Intel TBB is the production reference for task-arena NUMA grouping and adaptive grain partitioning. PCS borrows the per-domain deque group concept (Phase 9 mirrors TBB arena design) but replaces TBB's help-first default with work-first for better cache behavior in divide-and-conquer workloads.

Java ForkJoinPool demonstrates the CountedCompleter pattern that directly inspires PCS's pending_deps: AtomicUsize countdown (§8.6, I5). The JFP async mode (help-first, breadth-first) is available in PCS as SchedPolicy::HelpFirst for producer-consumer DAG pipelines.

Rayon is the closest Rust peer. PCS differs in three ways: (1) first-class DAG with cycle rejection vs. Rayon's implicit join-tree only; (2) NUMA-aware locality vs. Rayon's OS-determined placement; (3) compensation threads for blocking code vs. Rayon's "don't block" contract.

Go runtime demonstrates M:N cooperative scheduling with preemption on function entry, sysmon starvation detection, and per-P work queues. PCS does not implement M:N multiplexing (1:1 worker-to-OS-thread) because its target is CPU-bound compute, not I/O concurrency where M:N shines.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All numbers derive from §4 System Constraints. Assumptions are stated inline; revisit when §4 changes.

Baseline configuration (from §4)

  • num_threads = P = number of physical cores (e.g., P = 32 on a 2-socket Xeon)
  • Deque depth = D = 1024 slots per worker (Chase-Lev initial buffer)
  • Task body size = S = 64 bytes (Task struct: fn pointer + closure captures, cache-aligned)
  • Steal cost = C_steal = ~200 ns (one CAS + cache-line ping; measured on Xeon E5 class)
  • Task execution time = T_task = target ≥ 2000 ns (10× steal cost, from P6)
  • Max in-flight tasks = M = 64 × P (PoolConfig::max_in_flight default)

Step 1 — Live deque memory (per worker)

D slots × S bytes = 1 024 × 64 = 64 KB per worker deque buffer

At P=32 workers: 32 × 64 KB = 2 MB total deque memory. Negligible.

Step 2 — Peak in-flight task memory

M × S = 64 × 32 × 64 B = 131 072 B ≈ 128 KB task-struct memory

Closures captured on the heap add to this. A 1 KB mean closure: 128 KB × 16 = 2 MB. Acceptable.

Step 3 — Steal rate ceiling (no contention)

steal_rate_max = P / C_steal = 32 / 200 ns = 160 M steals/sec

In practice, steals are rare when task time >> steal cost (target T_task = 2000 ns):

steal_fraction ≈ C_steal / T_task = 200 / 2000 = 10%

At 10% steal fraction and 32 workers: 32 × (1/2 µs) × 0.10 = 1.6 M steals/sec. Well under the CAS bandwidth of a modern cache hierarchy.

Step 4 — Minimum task size for linear speedup

From P-B (Blumofe-Leiserson): E[time] ≤ T1/P + O(T∞). For P-fold speedup, T1/P must dominate T∞:

T∞ < T1/P  →  parallelism = T1/T∞ > P

Granularity constraint (from MM3): each leaf task must run ≥ 10× the steal cost:

T_task_min = 10 × C_steal = 10 × 200 ns = 2 µs

A task doing < 2 µs of work on this configuration will have steal overhead > 10% of task cost.

Step 5 — Memory bandwidth under parallel reduce

Target: 256 MB array, 8-byte doubles, one reduce pass.

BW_required = 256 MB / T_sequential
T_sequential = 256 MB / (8 B/elem × 500 M ops/s) ≈ 64 ms
At P=32: T_parallel ≈ 64 ms / 32 = 2 ms

At 2 ms and 256 MB touched: bandwidth = 256 MB / 2 ms = 128 GB/s. Dual-socket Xeon peak ≈ 120 GB/s. This is bandwidth-bound at P=32; adding more cores does not help. Mitigation: NUMA-local allocation (Phase 9) halves cross-socket traffic.

Step 6 — DAG cycle-check cost at submission

pcs-graph rejects cyclic DAGs at submit time with DFS (O(V + E)):

V = 100 000 nodes, E = 150 000 edges (sparse DAG)
DFS at 10 ns/edge visit = 150 000 × 10 ns = 1.5 ms

For a graph submitted once and executed in hours, 1.5 ms submit latency is acceptable. For graphs resubmitted at > 1 Hz with V > 100 k, consider incremental cycle detection or skip for trusted graphs.

Step 7 — Trace ring buffer overhead and capacity

Default ring size = 1 M spans × 40 bytes = 40 MB. At 10 M tasks/sec, ring fills in 100 ms and begins dropping. For production profiling, reduce to 100 k spans and sample at 1%.

Step 8 — Compensation thread bound

From ADR-27: K = 4× compensation factor. Maximum compensation threads = 4 × P = 128 at P=32. OS thread creation = ~10 µs each; burst cost to inject K compensation threads = 128 × 10 µs = 1.28 ms. Acceptable for blocking outliers; unacceptable if all tasks block simultaneously (design error, not scheduler fault).

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

Each question requires synthesis of at least two sections. The § reference shows where the authoritative answer lives.

  1. Why does the thief use Acquire on top when stealing, and what memory-model guarantee does that provide to the rest of the steal sequence? (§8.3; P-C sec. 3) — The Acquire load on top synchronizes with the Release store that the previous owner performed when decrementing bottom after a pop. This establishes a happens-before from the owner's slot-clear to the thief's slot-read, preventing the thief from reading a slot the owner cleared after the last steal.

  2. What is the exact condition under which steal() returns Empty vs Retry, and why does the retry path exist? (§8.3 Chase-Lev steal algorithm; P-A sec. 4) — Retry when the CAS on top fails (another thief won the race for the same slot); Empty when bottom ≤ top after loading both. The retry path exists because a stale top read can make a non-empty deque appear empty momentarily.

  3. How does the Blumofe-Leiserson makespan bound apply when tasks block on Future::wait()? (Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model step 3; §8.8; Phase 8; P-B sec. 2) — The B&L bound assumes processors are never idle while work exists. A blocking wait() violates this by idling a worker; Phase 12 compensation threads restore the invariant by injecting a spare worker when blocking is detected, preserving E[time] ≤ T1/P + O(T∞).

  4. A DAG has a node with in-degree 100. What is the atomic ordering required on the pending_deps decrement, and why is Relaxed insufficient? (§8.6; Phase 5; I5; MM8) — Release is required on the final decrement (the one that drives pending_deps to 0) so that all predecessor-task results are visible to the successor that reads them. Relaxed would allow the scheduler to push the successor as ready before its inputs are visible.

  5. Why does the scope Scope<'s> use an invariant lifetime 's rather than a covariant one? (§8.5; Phase 4; B2 Ch.19; I8) — Invariance prevents the caller from shortening the scope's lifetime via subtyping, which would allow spawned tasks to outlive the data they borrow. A covariant 's would unsoundly allow &'short T to be passed where &'long T is required.

  6. What prevents a compensation thread from being spawned when Scope::spawn is called from outside a worker context? (Phase 12; ADR-27) — block_in_place is only callable from within a worker-context closure. External submit goes through the Injector; external threads that call submit are not workers and cannot trigger compensation injection.

  7. In the work-first scheduling policy, why does the spawning thread always execute the child rather than the continuation? (§8.5; ADR-21; P-D; P-F) — Work-first runs the child immediately so the spawner's stack frame (which is the continuation) is at the bottom of the deque, available for stealing by any idle worker. This maximizes the amount of coarse-grained continuations exposed to thieves, matching the FIFO-steal principle (MM2).

  8. What prevents two thieves from both successfully stealing the same task? (§8.3; Phase 2; P-A sec. 4) — The steal CAS on top is the exclusive gate: only the thief whose CAS succeeds claims the slot. All others get Retry. The monotonic epoch in top prevents ABA wrap-around from making two successful steals appear on the same slot index (I3).

  9. The pending_deps counter can be decremented by multiple threads simultaneously. What ordering ensures the successor task is pushed to the ready queue exactly once? (§8.6; I5; Phase 5; MM8) — fetch_sub(1, Release) followed by checking the returned value: only the thread that observes the transition 1→0 pushes the task. All other decrements return values > 0 and do nothing, making the push single-occurrence without a mutex.

  10. How does parallel_scan (prefix scan) achieve O(log N) depth on P processors, and what associativity constraint must the user-supplied operator satisfy? (§8.7; Phase 6; B4 Ch.7; P-L) — The Blelloch two-phase scan (up-sweep + down-sweep) requires strict associativity: op(op(a,b),c) == op(a,op(b,c)). Floating-point addition is not associative in IEEE 754; results will differ from sequential scan. Users must acknowledge this or use integer accumulation.

  11. When a NUMA-local steal fails, in what order does the scheduler attempt cross-domain steals, and how does this relate to the NUMA topology graph? (§8.10; Phase 9; P-K; ADR-11) — The locality scorer ranks victims by NUMA distance (hop count in the topology graph): same core-complex first, same socket second, remote socket last. This mirrors TBB arena affinity (Comparative Architecture Table) and limits cross-socket cache-line migration.

  12. What is the ABA problem in the context of the Chase-Lev deque, and how does the monotonic epoch field in top defeat it? (§8.3; Phase 2; I3; MM5) — ABA: top wraps to a previously-seen value after the buffer cycles, causing a thief's stale CAS to succeed on a recycled slot. The epoch field occupies the upper half of the top word, incremented on every steal. A stale CAS now fails because the epoch has advanced even if the index matches.

  13. If PoolConfig::num_threads is set to 1, what changes in observable behavior vs. a sequential execution? (§8.1; Phase 1; Comparative Architecture Table) — Steal path is dead (no victims exist); pop() always succeeds; no NUMA grouping; block_in_place can still inject one compensation thread. The deque is used but all tasks run on the single worker in LIFO order. Result is deterministic and matches single-threaded output (Phase 14 conformance test fork_join.rs).

  14. What does "structured concurrency" mean in the context of Scope<'s>, and how does it differ from spawning threads without a scope? (§8.5; Phase 4; I4; I7; B2 Ch.19) — Structured concurrency guarantees that all tasks spawned in Scope::spawn complete before the scope itself returns, enforced by the borrow-checker lifetime 's. Unscoped thread spawning (std::thread::spawn) requires 'static data and has no completion guarantee at the call site.

  15. Why is the ChaseLevDeque buffer grown but never shrunk, and what are the memory-budget implications at scale? (§8.3; Phase 2; MM5; Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model step 1) — Shrinking requires reclaiming a buffer that a thief might still hold a pointer to; safe reclamation requires epoch-based or hazard-pointer reclamation, which is not implemented. The memory cost is bounded by the maximum recursion depth × task size per worker (Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model: ≤ 64 KB per worker at D=1024).

  16. What happens to tasks in a Scope when a panic occurs in one of the spawned closures? (§8.5; Phase 4; S8; I8; Comparative Architecture Table) — The panicking task is caught by catch_unwind inside the task executor. The scope is marked poisoned. Remaining tasks that have not started are dropped (not executed). The scope waits for in-flight tasks to finish, then re-panics at the scope() call site, propagating the original payload.

  17. How does the steal-half variant differ from single-steal, and in what workloads does it dominate? (§8.9; Phase 7; P-E sec. 5) — Steal-half migrates half the victim's deque in a single operation, amortizing CAS overhead across multiple tasks. It dominates in producer-heavy workloads where one worker generates a large batch of fine-grained tasks faster than a single thief can drain them; single-steal under-utilizes idle cores in that case.

  18. What coordination ensures the Injector does not return a task to two workers simultaneously? (§8.2; Phase 1; P-J; ADR-06) — The Injector is a Michael-Scott queue (P-J): dequeue uses a CAS on the head pointer; only the thread whose CAS succeeds claims the node. All others retry or see empty. The MSQ's linearization point is the head CAS.

  19. How does the loom model checker verify the Chase-Lev deque correctness, and what does it not verify? (§5 R9; Phase 14; ADR-18) — loom exhaustively schedules every possible interleaving of atomic operations within the LOOM_MAX_BRANCHES budget, checking for data races, assertion failures, and deadlocks. It does not verify: (1) correctness under weak memory models weaker than the C11 model loom implements (ARM relaxed); (2) performance properties; (3) behavior under more than loom::MAX_THREADS threads.

  20. What invariant must hold on bottom such that the owner's pop() is wait-free? (§8.3; Phase 2; P-A sec. 3; P-C) — bottom is written only by the owner; no thief ever writes it. The owner loads bottom without a CAS, making the pop path a simple decrement + conditional load, which completes in O(1) with no retry. Wait-freedom is guaranteed by the single-writer invariant on bottom.

  21. In the pending_deps DAG model, what prevents a node from being pushed to the ready queue twice if two predecessors complete in a tight race? (§8.6; I5; MM8; Phase 5) — fetch_sub is atomic; it returns the previous value. Only the decrement that returns 1 (driving the counter to 0) triggers the push. Decrements returning > 1 do nothing. The atomic fetch_sub itself is the serialization point; no lock or secondary check is needed.

  22. What constraint does the WorkFirst policy impose on the task-tree memory footprint that HelpFirst does not? (§8.5; ADR-21; P-D sec. 4; MM6) — Work-first produces depth-first execution: at any point, the open frames on the call stack correspond to the current path from root to leaf. Memory footprint is O(T∞ × frame_size). Help-first produces breadth-first execution: all sibling tasks can be in flight simultaneously, giving O(leaves × frame_size). For balanced binary trees, help-first uses O(T1) space; work-first uses O(log T1).

  23. How does the xorshift RNG in pcs-worker affect fairness of victim selection, and why is cryptographic randomness not used? (§8.3; Phase 3; ADR-08) — Xorshift provides a fast, deterministic, period-2^64−1 sequence that uniformly covers all victim indices over time, ensuring no worker is perpetually un-targeted. Cryptographic RNG (e.g., rand::os) costs ~100 ns/sample — the same order as the steal itself — making it unacceptably expensive on every steal attempt.

  24. What is the scheduling overhead model for a parallel_for over N elements split to grain G on P workers? (§8.7; Phase 6; Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model; B5 Ch.27) — Split overhead: O(N/G) spawns × spawn cost. Steal overhead: O(N/G / P) steals × steal cost. Total overhead: (N/G) × spawn_ns + (N/(G×P)) × steal_ns. Setting G = max(1, N/(P × K)) for K > 10 keeps the number of spawns to K×P — the adaptive split heuristic (Phase 6).

  25. What exactly does the Release fence in the buffer-resize path of ChaseLevDeque guarantee, and what would break without it? (§8.3; Phase 2; P-A; P-C) — The fence publishes all writes to the new buffer slots before the owner stores the new buffer pointer with Release. A thief that loads the buffer pointer with Acquire and then loads a slot will see fully initialized slot data. Without the fence, a thief could observe the new buffer pointer but read uninitialized slot bytes, causing a use-after-free on the old buffer.

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

Alt-1: Centralized Work-Sharing Queue

External                ┌──────────────────────┐
Submitters ──push──►   │  Global MPMC Queue    │
                        │  (bounded, lock-free) │
                        └──────────┬───────────┘
                     pop (FIFO)    │   pop (FIFO)
                        ▼          ▼          ▼
                    Worker 0   Worker 1   Worker P-1

How it works: one central Michael-Scott or bounded MPMC queue; all workers pop from one end; submitters push from the other. No per-worker deques. No stealing.

Regime where it wins: producer-consumer pipelines with P ≤ 4 workers and coarse tasks (T_task >> 10 µs). At low parallelism the cache-ping overhead of the central queue is dominated by task runtime. Go's work queue before Go 1.14 used this model for goroutines (R5).

Real system: Java ThreadPoolExecutor with a LinkedBlockingQueue. Simple, well-understood, straightforward backpressure.

Why rejected here: the global queue is a serialization bottleneck at P > 8 — all workers contend on the same head pointer. Each pop is O(1) but carries a full cache-line invalidation broadcast. Chase-Lev's per-worker deque eliminates this entirely (ADR-06, ADR-05).

Alt-2: Static Graph Partitioning (Bulk-Synchronous Parallel)

DAG (V nodes, E edges)
        │
  ┌─────▼──────┐
  │  Partition  │   offline: assign each node to worker_id
  │   (METIS)  │
  └────┬────┬──┘
       │    │
  Worker 0  Worker 1  ...  Worker P-1
  (owns      (owns           (owns
  nodes 0-K) nodes K-2K)    nodes 2K-V)
  │           │               │
  └──BSP sync point (barrier)──┘

How it works: statically partition the task graph among P workers before execution begins (e.g., using METIS or a heuristic BFS cut). Workers execute their partition; a global barrier synchronizes after each superstep.

Regime where it wins: highly regular DAGs (grid stencils, sparse-matrix multiplication) where the graph structure is known offline, communication is symmetric, and task cost variance is low. Charm++ and MPI+OpenMP codes use this model.

Real system: Bulk Synchronous Parallel (BSP) as implemented in Pregel (Google) and GraphX (Spark).

Why rejected here: task cost variance in general fork-join workloads is high; imbalance at the partition boundary causes 1/P workers to idle at every barrier. Dynamic work-stealing adapts automatically. Static partitioning also requires the full DAG to be known at partition time, which violates Phase 4's incremental Scope::spawn API.

Alt-3: Actor / Message-Passing Scheduler

Actor 0 ──msg──► Actor 1 ──msg──► Actor 2
   ▲                │
   └────────────────┘
   (each actor has a private mailbox; scheduler delivers messages)

How it works: each concurrent unit is an actor with a private FIFO mailbox. No shared memory. The scheduler delivers messages; actors process one message at a time. Erlang/BEAM, Akka, and Pony use this model.

Regime where it wins: fault-tolerant distributed systems, event-driven I/O servers, systems requiring hot-code reload. Actor isolation makes crash containment cheap (supervisor trees). The BEAM VM achieves sub-millisecond GC pauses by collecting per-actor heaps.

Real system: Erlang/BEAM with reduction-counted preemption; Actix-web (Rust) for HTTP actors.

Why rejected here: actors impose a serialization point on every shared-state access — a message-send to a shared counter is orders of magnitude slower than an AtomicU64::fetch_add. For CPU-bound parallel computation over shared arrays (parallel reduce, parallel scan), message-passing adds per-element message overhead that dwarfs task cost. The project's target workload (§3) is shared-memory compute, not isolated event processing.

Alt-4: Async Task Runtime (Green-Threads / M:N)

M async tasks (Future state machines, each with .await yield points)
        │  multiplexed onto
        ▼
N OS threads (N = num_cpus; each thread runs an executor loop)
        │  a task that hits Poll::Pending is parked;
        ▼  another ready task runs on the same OS thread
(no task ever occupies an OS thread while waiting on I/O)

How it works: M:N multiplexing of lightweight async tasks onto N OS threads. Each task is a Future state machine that yields at .await points. The executor (Tokio, async-std) parks a task by returning Poll::Pending; another task runs on the same thread.

Regime where it wins: I/O-bound workloads where tasks spend most time waiting on network, disk, or timers. Tokio achieves millions of concurrent connections on a few OS threads. For latency-sensitive microservices, async is the correct model.

Real system: Tokio (R3); smol; async-std.

Why rejected here: CPU-bound parallel tasks do not have natural .await yield points — they run to completion. Forcing artificial yields to enable task switching adds per-yield overhead (state-machine transitions, poll overhead) without providing any parallelism benefit. Work-stealing on OS threads (1:1) gives CPU-bound code uninterrupted execution on a pinned core, which is strictly better. Phase 12 compensation threads handle the rare case of a CPU worker blocking in I/O.

Deferred Features Register

Features below are explicitly out of scope for the current 14-phase plan. Each entry names the phase where it could be inserted, the ADR that records the deferral decision, and the minimum prerequisite condition for revisiting.

# Feature Deferred From Earliest Phase ADR Revisit Condition
DF-01 Incremental / streaming DAG (nodes added after submit) Phase 5 Phase 15+ ADR-14 pcs-graph stabilized; use-case with streaming node arrival proven by benchmark
DF-02 Work-stealing across process boundaries (distributed pool) Phase 9 Phase 16+ ADR-11 Single-machine pool has 100% test coverage; network transport chosen
DF-03 Persistent task graph checkpointing / replay Phase 5 Phase 15+ ADR-14 pcs-trace replay mode complete (Phase 14); serialization format stable
DF-04 WebAssembly / WASI target (wasm32-wasi) Phase 1 Phase 15+ ADR-02 libc affinity APIs stubbed; WASM thread support standardized (WASI threads proposal)
DF-05 GPU / CUDA task offload via pcs-gpu crate Phase 6 Phase 16+ ADR-02 CPU scheduler saturates all cores; GPU data-transfer overhead characterized
DF-06 Priority inheritance for blocking-task waiters Phase 10 Phase 15+ ADR-15 Compensation thread model (Phase 12) proven stable over 8 h soak
DF-07 Lock-free deque shrink (hazard-pointer reclamation) Phase 2 Phase 15+ ADR-04 Memory pressure on embedded targets demonstrated; EBR crate audited
DF-08 Async Rust integration (impl Future tasks in the pool) Phase 8 Phase 15+ ADR-22 Tokio compatibility layer benchmarked; poll_fn task wrapper designed
DF-09 Real-time scheduling class (SCHED_FIFO / SCHED_RR) Phase 1 Phase 16+ ADR-09 RT kernel available in CI; CAP_SYS_NICE policy defined
DF-10 DAG visualization UI (live Gantt / critical path render) Phase 13 Phase 15+ — pcs-trace span format stable; browser rendering target confirmed

DF-12 Priority lanes beyond two tiers (full N-level priority scheduling) Phase 10 Phase 15+ — Two-tier lane starvation measured in production workload; pcs-sched priority-inversion analysis complete
DF-13 Uncapped compensation thread pool (beyond the 2× num_threads cap) Phase 12 Phase 15+ ADR-27 8 h soak proves 2× cap insufficient under sustained blocking load; OS thread-limit headroom confirmed
DF-14 OpenTelemetry / Jaeger span export (external tracing backend integration) Phase 13 Phase 15+ — pcs-trace span schema frozen; OTel Rust SDK embedding overhead measured acceptable (< 5% of task cost)
DF-15 Full loom exhaustive model checking across all crates (not just pcs-deque) as a mandatory CI gate Phase 14 Phase 15+ ADR-17 CI budget allows a >30 min loom job; LOOM_MAX_BRANCHES raised beyond the current bounded-exploration limit

Glossary

Alphabetical. Each term is defined with operational precision relative to the pcs-* codebase.

Term Definition
ABA problem Deque hazard: top is CAS'd back to an old value after wrapping, causing a thief to believe a slot is valid when the buffer has cycled. Defeated by monotonic top counter (I3).
Affinity Binding a worker thread to a specific logical CPU or CpuSet via OS primitives (sched_setaffinity / pthread_setaffinity_np), preventing migration and preserving cache state.
Backpressure Mechanism in pcs-pool / Injector: external spawn blocks or returns Err when in-flight task count exceeds a configured bound (P10).
Barrier pcs-sync primitive: N participants call wait(); all block until all N have arrived; analogous to MPI_Barrier; used in bulk-synchronous parallel phases.
Bottom Owner-side atomic index of the Chase-Lev deque; incremented on push, decremented on pop. Written only by the owner thread.
Cancellation token Shared flag (AtomicBool) threaded into a task closure; long-running tasks check it cooperatively to abort early (Phase 11).
Chase-Lev deque Lock-free circular work-stealing deque from Chase & Lev (SPAA 2005); owner push/pop are wait-free; thief steal uses CAS on top. Core of pcs-deque.
Continuation stealing Scheduling variant where the continuation of a fork is stolen by the thief while the spawner executes the new child (HelpFirst policy in SchedPolicy).
CpuSet Bitmask of logical CPU ids; assigned per worker in PoolConfig; passed to OS affinity API in pcs-pool.
DAG Directed acyclic task graph; nodes are GraphNode instances; edges are Dependency records; managed in pcs-graph.
Dependency Edge (pred_id → succ_id) in the TaskGraph; completion of pred decrements succ's pending_deps; when pending_deps hits 0, succ becomes Ready (I5).
FIFO steal Thieves steal from the oldest (front) end of the victim's deque; oldest tasks tend to be the coarsest-grained and carry the most remaining work per steal (P3).
Fork-join Parallel pattern: fork spawns a child, join waits for both parent continuation and child to complete. Implemented in pcs-parallel via Scope and join().
Future<T> Handle to the result of an asynchronous task in pcs-sync; Future::wait() drives the scheduler rather than blocking the OS thread.
Granularity Expected cost of a single task in CPU cycles; must substantially exceed the cost of one steal operation to keep parallel overhead below the speedup gain (P6).
HelpFirst SchedPolicy variant: spawning thread immediately steals the continuation; promotes breadth-first execution; reduces memory footprint of task tree.
Injector<T> MPMC queue in pcs-pool used as the global overflow channel; external threads and over-full local deques push here; idle workers drain it.
Latch Single-use countdown synchronizer in pcs-sync; count decremented on task completion; threads waiting on the latch participate in scheduling rather than blocking.
LIFO pop Owner pops the most-recently-pushed task; accesses cache-hot data, smallest-grained subtasks, maximizing data locality (P3).
loom Model-checker crate (tokio-rs/loom) that exhaustively explores interleavings of atomic operations; used in Phase 14 to verify pcs-deque's CAS logic.
NUMA domain A group of CPU cores sharing a local memory bus; cross-domain memory accesses incur higher latency. pcs-numa groups workers by domain and biases stealing within-domain (Phase 9).
Over-decomposition Splitting work into many more tasks than cores; acceptable for load balance provided each task still exceeds steal cost (P4, P6).
Oversubscription Spawning more OS threads than hardware cores; causes thrashing and context-switch overhead. The pool avoids this by holding exactly num_threads worker threads (P4).
Owner The single worker thread that holds write access to its ChaseLevDeque bottom pointer; only the owner may push or pop.
Panic propagation A task that panics transitions to TaskState::Panicked; the panic payload is captured, re-raised at the enclosing Scope or join boundary (I7, Phase 11).
Parking A worker that finds no local work and fails to steal parks (blocks on a condvar / futex) to avoid busy-spinning; unparked when new work arrives on any deque (Phase 3).
pending_deps AtomicUsize on GraphNode; initialized to in-degree; each predecessor completion decrements it; reaching 0 triggers a ready-push to a worker deque (I5).
Producer In pcs-parallel, a splittable iterator source that can be halved recursively; mirrors rayon's UnindexedProducer / IndexedProducer.
Promise<T> Write side of the Future/Promise pair; fulfilled by the task that computes T; signals any waiting Future::wait().
Scan (prefix) Data-parallel prefix sum / exclusive-scan; requires two passes (reduce + distribute); implemented as a composed parallel primitive in pcs-parallel.
SchedPolicy Enum {WorkFirst, HelpFirst, LocalityAware}; selects continuation placement and victim preference strategy at pool construction time.
Scope<'s> Lexically-scoped fork-join region; tasks spawned within borrow 's; the Scope::wait() blocks the caller until all spawned tasks reach terminal state (I8).
Splitter The recursive halving function in pcs-parallel that divides a Producer's range at the midpoint until leaf granularity is reached.
Steal-half Variant where a thief steals half of the victim's deque in a single operation rather than one task; reduces steal-per-task overhead when deques are deep.
Straggler A task that remains Running or Pending after most peers are Done; causes load imbalance; detected via deque-depth gauges in pcs-metrics.
Structured concurrency Every task belongs to an enclosing Scope or join that guarantees completion before the parent returns; no detached or orphaned tasks (P5, I8).
Thief Any worker thread other than the owner attempting to steal from a victim's deque via CAS on top.
Top Thief-side atomic index of the Chase-Lev deque; incremented by a successful CAS steal. Monotonically increasing to defeat ABA (I3).
Unparking Signaling a parked worker (via condvar notify / futex wake) when new tasks are pushed to any deque or the Injector, resuming steal attempts.
Victim The worker whose deque a thief attempts to steal from; selected via random index or NUMA-local bias in pcs-worker.
WaitGroup pcs-sync primitive: counter decremented by done(); zero triggers wakeup of all waiters; analogous to Go sync.WaitGroup.
Work-first SchedPolicy variant: spawning thread immediately runs the new child; the continuation is left on the deque for potential stealing; maximizes locality of child data.
Work-stealing Scheduling strategy where idle workers steal tasks from busy workers' deques; provides load balancing without a central queue or global lock (P1, P8).
WorkFirst See Work-first.
XorShift Lightweight PRNG (xorshift64) carried per Worker; used for randomized victim selection without shared state or locking.

Appendix A — File Inventory

Files consistent with the repository layout in section 10. Path anchored at crate root where applicable.

Path Purpose

pcs-core/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; no threading deps; re-exports TaskId, Task, TaskState
pcs-core/src/lib.rs Public re-exports; crate-level doc
pcs-core/src/task.rs Task struct, TaskId(u64), AtomicTaskState wrapper
pcs-core/src/state.rs TaskState enum {Pending, Ready, Running, Done, Panicked}; encode/decode helpers
pcs-core/src/job.rs Boxed FnOnce() + Send job wrapper; inline-vs-heap storage threshold
pcs-core/src/error.rs PcsError enum; PanicPayload wrapper; spawn/submit error variants
pcs-deque/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; no_std compatible; atomic dep only
pcs-deque/src/lib.rs Public API: ChaseLevDeque<T>, Stealer<T>, steal result enum
pcs-deque/src/deque.rs Chase-Lev ring buffer; bottom/top AtomicUsize; push/pop/steal; buffer growth
pcs-deque/src/buffer.rs Circular buffer allocation; capacity-doubling; unsafe raw-ptr slot access
pcs-deque/tests/loom_deque.rs loom model-check: owner+1 thief interleavings; Phase 14 conformance
pcs-deque/tests/unit.rs Single-threaded LIFO/FIFO property tests; wrap-around; ABA cases
pcs-worker/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; depends on pcs-core, pcs-deque
pcs-worker/src/lib.rs Public: Worker struct, WorkerHandle, thread-local CURRENT_WORKER
pcs-worker/src/worker.rs Worker main loop: pop-local, steal-victim, park; exponential backoff
pcs-worker/src/steal.rs Victim selection: random XorShift + NUMA-local bias; steal-half variant
pcs-worker/src/rng.rs XorShift64 PRNG; deterministic seed in determinism mode
pcs-pool/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; depends on pcs-worker, pcs-numa; libc for affinity
pcs-pool/src/lib.rs Public: ThreadPool, PoolConfig, spawn(), submit()
pcs-pool/src/pool.rs Thread lifecycle: spawn N workers, pin affinity, shutdown join; Injector integration
pcs-pool/src/config.rs PoolConfig: num_threads, stack_size, affinity, policy; builder
pcs-pool/src/topology.rs Hardware topology detection: core count, NUMA node map, CpuSet per core
pcs-pool/src/injector.rs MPMC Injector<Task>; bounded capacity; backpressure on push
pcs-pool/tests/integration.rs Pool startup/shutdown; spawn/join correctness; panic propagation end-to-end
pcs-graph/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; depends on pcs-core, pcs-pool
pcs-graph/src/lib.rs Public: TaskGraph, GraphNode, NodeId, Dependency, add_node, add_edge, submit
pcs-graph/src/graph.rs DAG storage; pending_deps init; edge validation; cycle rejection (Kahn check)
pcs-graph/src/ready.rs Completion handler: decrement successors, push Ready nodes to worker deques
pcs-graph/tests/dag.rs Diamond deps, chain deps, fan-out/in; cycle rejection; completion correctness
pcs-parallel/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; depends on pcs-pool, pcs-sync
pcs-parallel/src/lib.rs Public: parallel_for, par_map, par_reduce, par_scan, Scope, join
pcs-parallel/src/scope.rs Scope<'s> fork-join: spawn borrows 's; wait drives scheduler; panic capture
pcs-parallel/src/join.rs join(a, b): push b, run a, pop-or-steal b; work-first semantics
pcs-parallel/src/producer.rs ParallelIterator, Producer trait; split_at; recursive halving; leaf threshold
pcs-parallel/src/splitter.rs Adaptive splitter: steal-pressure check; granularity floor; split-count cap
pcs-parallel/src/iter.rs Concrete producers: slice, range; map/filter adapters
pcs-parallel/src/reduce.rs par_reduce: recursive binary tree; associativity contract; float note
pcs-parallel/src/scan.rs par_scan (prefix): two-phase reduce+distribute; exclusive/inclusive variants
pcs-parallel/tests/parallel.rs Mergesort, sum, dot-product; sequential equivalence checks (I9)
pcs-sched/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; depends on pcs-pool, pcs-worker
pcs-sched/src/lib.rs Public: SchedPolicy enum; policy-dispatch hooks; locality scorer
pcs-sched/src/policy.rs WorkFirst / HelpFirst continuation placement; continuation-task routing
pcs-sched/src/locality.rs NUMA-local victim ranking; domain affinity scoring; steal-distance metric
pcs-sched/src/granularity.rs Auto-tuning: measure task duration vs steal cost; adjust split threshold
pcs-sync/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; depends on pcs-core; futex or condvar backend
pcs-sync/src/lib.rs Public: Future<T>, Promise<T>, Latch, Barrier, WaitGroup
pcs-sync/src/future.rs Future<T>/Promise<T>: atomic state; wait() drives worker loop; set() notifies
pcs-sync/src/latch.rs CountDownLatch: AtomicUsize; await_zero() participates in scheduling
pcs-sync/src/barrier.rs CyclicBarrier: N-phase; futex-based; worker loop participation on wait
pcs-sync/src/waitgroup.rs WaitGroup: add/done/wait; go-compatible semantics
pcs-sync/tests/sync.rs Future fulfillment; latch countdown; barrier rendezvous; WaitGroup drain
pcs-numa/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; libnuma or /sys/devices/system/node parsing; cfg(target_os)
pcs-numa/src/lib.rs Public: NumaNode, NumaDomain, numa_topology(), bind_thread()
pcs-numa/src/topology.rs Parse /sys NUMA topology; build domain→core map; fallback single-domain
pcs-numa/src/bind.rs Thread-to-NUMA binding; mbind for allocation policy; libnuma shim
pcs-numa/src/domain_group.rs Per-domain deque groups; within-domain Injector; cross-domain steal gate
pcs-metrics/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; depends on pcs-core; atomic counters only
pcs-metrics/src/lib.rs Public: StealStats, SchedMetrics, per-worker snapshot, aggregate
pcs-metrics/src/counters.rs AtomicU64 fields: steals_attempted, steals_won, tasks_run, park_count, idle_ns
pcs-metrics/src/histogram.rs Latency histogram: fixed-bucket log2 bins; task queue→start latency
pcs-metrics/src/report.rs Snapshot aggregation; JSON / human-readable render; Prometheus text format
pcs-trace/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; optional tracing feature; depends on pcs-core, pcs-metrics
pcs-trace/src/lib.rs Public: TaskSpan, TraceCollector, flamegraph_export(); toggle via env var
pcs-trace/src/span.rs Per-task span: task_id, worker_id, start_ns, end_ns, parent_id
pcs-trace/src/collector.rs Lock-free per-thread span buffer; drain to global sink on flush
pcs-trace/src/flamegraph.rs Convert span tree to Inferno/flamegraph format; fold + write to file
pcs-cli/Cargo.toml Crate manifest; bench binary; criterion dev-dep
pcs-cli/src/main.rs CLI entry: select benchmark by name; configure pool; run; print timing
pcs-cli/src/mergesort.rs Parallel mergesort benchmark: split-merge via Scope; correctness check
pcs-cli/src/nbody.rs N-body force computation: parallel_for over particle pairs; energy check
pcs-cli/src/bfs.rs Parallel BFS on random graph: frontier expansion; level-synchronous
pcs-cli/src/matmul.rs Matrix multiply: tiled parallel_for; numerical equivalence vs serial
pcs-cli/benches/scaling.rs Criterion: task throughput and speedup sweep 1..N cores; steal rate
pcs-cli/benches/overhead.rs Spawn+join microbench; steal cost; deque push/pop/steal latency
pcs-cli/tests/conformance.rs Sequential vs parallel output equality for all four benchmark kernels
benchmarks/benches/deque_throughput.rs Standalone criterion harness: raw push/pop/steal throughput, isolated from pcs-cli
benchmarks/benches/spawn_overhead.rs Scope::spawn overhead at N=1..1000 nested spawns
benchmarks/benches/scaling.rs Cross-crate scaling sweep independent of pcs-cli/benches/scaling.rs (library-level API)
benchmarks/benches/steal_rate.rs Steal attempted/won ratio under synthetic imbalance generators
benchmarks/benches/parallel_reduce.rs par_reduce throughput across element types and operator costs
conformance/tests/fork_join.rs join(a,b) sequential-equivalence conformance suite (Phase 4 exit gate)
conformance/tests/dag_order.rs TaskGraph completion-order conformance suite (Phase 5 exit gate)
conformance/tests/reduce_associative.rs I9 associativity conformance for parallel_reduce/parallel_scan
conformance/tests/panic_propagation.rs I7 panic re-raise conformance across Scope and TaskGraph boundaries
conformance/tests/scope_lifetime.rs I8 compile-fail (trybuild) suite proving Scope<'s> rejects use-after-scope
conformance/tests/cancellation.rs Cooperative cancellation conformance (ADR-12, Phase 11 exit gate)
fuzz/fuzz_targets/fuzz_deque.rs cargo-fuzz target: arbitrary push/pop/steal sequences on pcs-deque (ADR-18)
fuzz/fuzz_targets/fuzz_graph.rs cargo-fuzz target: arbitrary node/edge sequences on pcs-graph, cycle rejection
fuzz/fuzz_targets/fuzz_scope.rs cargo-fuzz target: arbitrary nested Scope::spawn / panic sequences
examples/quickstart.rs Minimal ThreadPool + parallel_for usage example
examples/dag_pipeline.rs TaskGraph example: multi-stage pipeline with fan-out/fan-in
examples/parallel_search.rs Fork-join example: parallel tree/graph search with early termination
examples/numa_affinity.rs pcs-numa example: per-domain injector usage and affinity binding
templates/new_crate/ Scaffold directory copied when adding a new pcs-* crate to the workspace
templates/adr_template.md Blank ADR template matching the six-column ADR schema used in the Tech Stack ADR Table (§9)
scripts/gen_flamegraph.sh Wraps pcs-trace::export_flamegraph + inferno-flamegraph into one command

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Timeline anchor: total ~28 weeks from Phase 1 start to Phase 14 soak complete. Phase 0 (project setup, workspace scaffold, CI) is a prerequisite row.

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
0 — Setup Workspace Cargo.toml, CI pipeline (GitHub Actions), lint/fmt gates, seed crate skeletons 0.5 wk Wrong crate boundaries, CI flakiness All crates compile; CI green on empty stubs
1 — Thread pool + topology ThreadPool spawning N pinned workers; PoolConfig; hardware topology; clean shutdown 2 wk OS affinity API portability (Linux vs macOS); NUMA detection fallback Workers start/stop cleanly; affinity verified via /proc/self/status; topology parsed
2 — Chase-Lev deque ChaseLevDeque<T> with correct C11 orderings; Stealer handle; growable buffer 2 wk Memory ordering bugs (relaxed vs acquire/release); ABA on wrap-around loom exhaustive check passes; unit LIFO/FIFO tests pass; no data races under ThreadSanitizer
3 — Task model + steal loop Task = FnOnce + AtomicTaskState; spawn; worker pop/steal/park loop; unpark 2 wk Park/unpark race (miss wake); exponential backoff too aggressive Spawn+run correctness; no missed wakeups under loom; idle CPU near zero when no work
4 — Fork-join join(a,b) work-first; Scope<'s> lifetime; panic capture; recursive divide-and-conquer 2 wk Scope borrow-checker edge cases; panic double-frees Mergesort parallel == serial; panics re-raise at join boundary; Scope does not return early (I8)
5 — Task graph / DAG TaskGraph; pending_deps countdown; cycle rejection; completion latch 2 wk Cycle in user graph; race on pending_deps reaching 0 twice Diamond/chain/fan tests pass; cycle rejected with error; pending_deps never underflows
6 — Data-parallel primitives parallel_for, par_map, par_reduce, par_scan; Producer split; granularity threshold 2.5 wk Scan two-phase synchronization; float reassociation divergence Sequential equivalence for reduce/scan (I9); throughput > serial baseline at ≥2 threads
7 — Scheduling policy + locality WorkFirst / HelpFirst / LocalityAware; victim NUMA bias; granularity auto-tune 1.5 wk Policy switch regresses existing benchmarks; locality bias reduces steal balance Policy selectable at runtime; NUMA-local steal rate > 70% on two-socket machine; no speedup regression
8 — Futures / promises Future<T>/Promise<T>; wait() drives scheduler; continuation chains 1.5 wk wait() re-entry (nested wait); fulfillment before wait() call Future::wait() does not block OS thread; continuation fires exactly once; no starvation under loom
9 — NUMA-aware scheduling Per-domain deque groups; allocate on local domain; cross-domain steal as last resort 2 wk Unavailable libnuma on test hosts; topology detection inaccuracy NUMA locality tests on two-socket host; cross-domain steals < 15% of total; CI skip if no NUMA
10 — Priority + fairness + backpressure Priority lanes; Injector bounded; backpressure on submit(); fairness guard 1.5 wk Priority inversion; backpressure deadlock when all workers blocked in user code High-priority tasks run before low under load; submit() returns Err on saturation; no starvation detected over 60 s soak
11 — Cancellation + panic propagation CancellationToken; Scope poisoning; structured shutdown drain/abort 1.5 wk Token check latency; partial-graph cancellation leaving nodes Pending Cancelled task stops within 10 ms of token set; shutdown leaves zero Pending/Running tasks (I10)
12 — Blocking-task handling Compensation thread injection; blocking detection; bounded compensation pool 1.5 wk Unbounded thread growth; deadlock when all workers block in join Join does not deadlock when all workers block; compensation threads capped; ThreadSanitizer clean
13 — Observability StealStats counters; latency histogram; flamegraph export; tracing toggle 1 wk Tracing overhead disrupts benchmark baselines; flamegraph format incompatibility Metrics overhead < 2% on mergesort bench; flamegraph renders in Inferno; counters monotonic
14 — Determinism + bench + conformance Deterministic replay mode; criterion scaling benches; loom deque recheck; soak 8 h 2 wk Determinism mode misses scheduler nondeterminism; bench variance > 5% Determinism mode reproduces identical output on re-run; linear speedup to ≥4 cores on all kernels; 8 h soak zero crashes
Total Full pcs-* workspace, all primitives, observability, benches 28 wk — All phases exited; CI green; loom+ThreadSanitizer clean; soak passed
